#5135. Binance Finds 'Additional Points of Breach' in $6.3B Hack

Binance Finds 'Additional Points of Breach' in $6.3B Hack

Binance has uncovered "additional points of breach" in Thursday's security incident, which it estimates cost around $6.3 billion, the exchange said on Friday, after initially pointing to a compromised engineer's laptop.

In an announcement late Thursday, Binance said a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop may have allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions. "We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak," the exchange said.

Investigation update: Our initial investigation suggested that the security incident may have involved a compromised laptop, however our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach.

This was a complex attack, and we are doing all we can in coordination… pic.twitter.com/FQQ3vKT6GU

Binance CEO Richard Teng said on Friday that the attacker "did not simply use one engineer's permissions, and this was not a single 'one-click' action," describing "a more complex attack process." "Our investigation has not attributed responsibility to any individual," he wrote.

"This was a complex attack," Binance said in its latest update, adding that it is still gathering the facts. Outflows stopped when it paused withdrawals, and it remains on track to resume them in 45 hours, the exchange said.

I want to speak to you directly about what happened.

Bottom line - your money is safe, and we're covering every loss.

Our initial investigation suggests this security incident may have started with a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop. That gave an attacker access to a…

— Richard Teng (@_RichardTeng) October 30, 2026

About $3 billion of the affected assets has been frozen onchain with the help of blockchain partners, Binance said. The exchange said it will cover all losses from its SAFU fund, an emergency reserve for users, and from company funds, and that "user funds are safe."

Binance initially suspended deposits and withdrawals, revoked the affected address authorization, and is working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the funds. By Friday morning, deposits were "operating as normal," Teng said, while withdrawals remain suspended as the exchange completes "a security revamp of the withdrawal service and its related addresses."

"We'll share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and every fact is verified—and publish an updated Proof of Reserves once remediation is complete," Teng wrote.

Blockchain analytics firm Arkham put the haul at $6.01 billion, taken from Binance hot wallets on nine blockchains between 4:10 p.m. and 7:05 p.m. ET on Thursday, it said in a tweet. Issuers had frozen $2.80 billion of it, but $2.9 billion in Ethereum, Bitcoin, Tron, Solana and other tokens could still be moved, according to Arkham.

The attacker has kept moving funds. Early Friday, the wallets sent $38.82 million in Ethereum to a new address, while still holding $1.12 billion in ETH, Arkham said. Hours later, a Tron address tagged by Arkham as belonging to the attacker sent about $2.1 million in TRX to nine new addresses in 14 transfers, according to Arkham data.

Stablecoin issuers moved quickly as the funds left Binance. Tether and Circle froze about 975.1 million USDT and 823 million USDC held in the main receiving wallet, worth about $1.8 billion, Decrypt reported on Thursday, when an onchain tally put the drain at $5.57 billion. Binance co-founder Changpeng "CZ" Zhao thanked the two issuers for their "quick freeze."

As of Friday morning, Arkham's entity page for the attacker showed holdings of about 18,270 BTC, 488,364 ETH, 663.6 million TRX and 970,551 SOL, alongside frozen stablecoin balances.

#5133. Trezor reports suspected hardware implant in Safe 5 sold by CryptoBilis

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Trezor has not received the device but is treating the risk as credible, urging customers who used units sold by CryptoBilis since March 2026 to move their funds.

Trezor urged customers who purchased hardware wallets from reseller CryptoBilis since March 2026 to migrate their funds immediately, following a responsible disclosure alleging a physical hardware implant in a Safe 5 device.

The Czech hardware wallet maker removed CryptoBilis from its authorized reseller network earlier this month following reports of tampered Ledger devices sold through the same distributor.

In a blog post published Friday, Trezor said it received the disclosure on Oct. 28 regarding a unit bought in August 2026 through Tokopedia. The company has not received the device for in-depth examination but it interviewed the customer by video call and is treating the report as credible, it added.

"We'd rather warn people now than wait for confirmation and find out someone lost their funds in the meantime," Trezor CCO Danny Sanders said in a statement to The Block. "If you bought a Trezor from CryptoBilis since March, move your funds to a new wallet and contact our Support team, and we'll send you a new device."

According to the post, the reported implant consists of an additional circuit board fitted between the device's main board and screen. It contains a cellular module and antenna that can reportedly read information displayed on the screen, including a wallet's recovery phrase, and transmit it over a mobile network.

Trezor said it began inspecting devices purchased from the reseller after removing it from its network. The company has received four customer devices for examination so far and said it found no signs of tampering in those units.

Rival wallet maker Ledger previously confirmed physical tampering in signers sold through CryptoBilis, while OneKey similarly severed ties with the reseller, froze new approvals, and offered free replacements.

For Trezor's part, no user funds loss has been reported. Onchain analysts estimated losses tied to the Ledger incident at more than $86 million across Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Tron, but the wallet maker has not confirmed those figures.

Meanwhile, the CryptoBilis incident is not the first security headache for Trezor this year. In August, a breach at shipping provider ShipMonk exposed personal data belonging to about 13,700 customers.

In September, Trezor disclosed that another approximately 67,000 U.S. customers were affected by the same breach. Also in the same month, the company said a breach at its third-party email provider had enabled phishing messages to be sent from Trezor's official domain.

Blockchain analytics company Chainalysis said it was targeted in a cybersecurity incident, similar to those suffered recently by other platforms.

#5130. Binance Reopens Withdrawals as Hack Recovery Enters Second Week

Binance has reopened withdrawals after its $6.3 billion North Korea hack, covered 95% of losses, and has reserves for the remaining 5%, with post-mortem reviews to stay open.

Binance restarted customer withdrawals on Sunday, four days after hackers drained roughly $6.3 billion from its hot wallets in the largest crypto theft on record.

The exchange reopened in stages over the weekend, starting with bitcoin and expanding to 15 networks. The move marked the first real test of whether the platform could absorb a loss of that size without freezing its customers out for longer.

The recovery push began Saturday. In its third recovery update, Binance said it had replenished 90% of the impacted amount with company funds. The exchange drew on part of its Secure Asset Fund for Users, a portion of its futures insurance fund and its corporate treasury to buy replacement tokens.

The futures fund contribution drew particular attention, since Binance described the remaining balance as a regulatory requirement. Binance also published a new proof-of-reserves snapshot dated October 31, giving users a baseline to check their balances against.

Sunday’s reopening began at 06:00 UTC with BTC. Binance then added ETH, BNB Smart Chain, Solana, XRP, Dogecoin, Tron, Polygon, Avalanche, Arbitrum, Base, Optimism, Zcash, TON and Sui. Between 06:00 and 15:30 UTC, the exchange processed about $4.65 billion in withdrawals against roughly $1.01 billion in deposits.

That produced a net outflow near $3.64 billion, equal to about 2.1% of the $171 billion in assets Binance reported in its late-October proof of reserves.

Deposits stayed open throughout the rollout, and the exchange said temporary delays were possible during the initial surge in volume.

Binance said its Sunday update raised coverage to 95% of the impacted amount. The remaining 5% sits in less liquid tokens outside the top ten assets on its proof-of-reserves list. Sourcing those tokens at the required scale takes more time, and the exchange said it is working with industry partners and over-the-counter desks to complete the purchases. Binance has committed to covering the full amount, and it has repeated that users will not bear the financial impact of the theft.

On Monday, Binance said the full zero-knowledge proof for the October 31 snapshot is complete. Users holding BTC, ETH, USDT, BNB, SOL, USDC, XRP, USD1, RLUSD or ZEC can run the exchange’s verifier to confirm their balances are backed at least 1:1.

The verifier is open source, and the exchange has pointed users to a step-by-step guide.

The exchange plans another snapshot covering more tokens on November 4, with a proof expected to take 40 to 50 hours to generate. A further withdrawal phase is scheduled for Tuesday, and the exchange said it would share details once the rollout is ready.

On the law enforcement front, the FBI has attributed the attack to North Korea, and the U.S. Secret Service is working with Circle to trace and freeze funds. Tether reported about $1.57 billion in USDT frozen, and Circle said it had frozen more than $800 million in USDC in coordination with authorities.

The market reacted sharply to the breach. Bitcoin fell about 5% to below $77,000 in the hours after the attack, and shares of Coinbase,Robinhood, Strategy, and Strive all declined as investors weighed the risk of exchange-level losses across the sector.

Binance has said it found additional points of breach beyond the initial compromise, which it originally attributed to a single engineer’s laptop. The exchange has not yet published a full post-mortem explaining how the attackers prepared and executed the operation, which some estimates suggest took at least 36 hours of groundwork.

The destination of roughly $3.3 billion in stolen funds that were not frozen has also not been fully explained, and the exchange has not detailed what it expects to recover from that pool. Investigators continue to track wallet activity across ten blockchains.

#5129. Trezor Warns CryptoBilis Buyers After Report of Hardware Implant in Safe 5

Trezor Warns CryptoBilis Buyers After Report of Hardware Implant in Safe 5

Hardware wallet manufacturer Trezor has warned customers who bought its devices from reseller CryptoBilis to move their funds, after a buyer reported finding a hardware implant inside a Trezor Safe 5, the company said on Friday.

A customer in Indonesia reported the implant through Trezor's responsible disclosure program on October 28, according to a company blog post. The Safe 5 was bought from CryptoBilis through Indonesian e-commerce platform Tokopedia in August 2026.

We've been made aware, through responsible disclosure, of a single hardware implant in a Trezor Safe 5 device bought from the reseller CryptoBilis in August 2026.

No CryptoBilis buyer has reported a loss of funds to us.

IMPORTANT: If you bought a Trezor from… pic.twitter.com/TiaKfHBnX2

The report describes an extra circuit board fitted between the device's main board and its screen, with a cellular module and an antenna. According to the report, which Trezor said it has not verified, the board reads what the screen shows, including the wallet backup, and sends it out over a mobile network whenever the device is plugged in.

Trezor has not yet received the device, but said it is treating the report as credible based on the customer's description and a video interview. "What we've seen looks sophisticated enough that we decided to warn customers now," it said. "Waiting for lab confirmation would leave CryptoBilis buyers exposed in the meantime."

No CryptoBilis buyer has reported a loss of funds to Trezor, the company said.

Trezor urged anyone who bought one of its wallets from CryptoBilis from March 2026 onwards to move their funds to a newly created wallet with a new recovery phrase as soon as possible, and not to reuse the old phrase on any device. Buyers who have not yet set up their device should not use it. Affected customers can keep their CryptoBilis device and contact Trezor Support for a replacement.

Trezor removed CryptoBilis as a reseller on October 9, after rival hardware wallet manufacturer Ledger began investigating losses among customers who bought its devices from the Southeast Asian reseller. Trezor has since inspected four of its devices bought from CryptoBilis, a Safe 7, two Safe 5s and a Safe 3, and found no evidence of tampering.

Ledger's Donjon security team found an implant in Ledger devices sold through CryptoBilis that reads the 24-word recovery phrase from the screen during setup and sends it out over LTE. Trezor's blog says the newly reported implant "appears to operate similarly," though the company added that it won't draw comparisons while investigations are ongoing and doesn't know who is behind it.

Ledger has said that all confirmed losses involve devices bought from CryptoBilis, and asked resellers in seven Asian markets to pull its devices. One of the largest victims was a scammer who lost more than 60 BTC, worth about $4.8 million, according to blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis.

Trezor has also had to warn customers about partner failures before. In August, it said a breach at shipping provider ShipMonk had exposed the names, phone numbers and home addresses of nearly 14,000 U.S. customers, a figure that grew by another 67,000 in September.

#5127. Coinbase Q3 net income jumps 66% as prediction markets gain traction

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Bernstein analysts said Coinbase's expanding prediction markets and derivatives businesses helped offset weaker spot trading activity.

Coinbase Coinbase Global, Inc. (COIN) reported a sharp jump in third-quarter net income to $719 million, as its expanding prediction markets and derivatives businesses helped offset weaker spot trading activity, analysts said.

According to the exchange's earnings report released Thursday, the company generated $1.22 billion in total revenue, broadly flat from the previous quarter but down 35% year over year.

The company's net income rose 66% year on year to $719 million from $433 million in the same period last year. The results included a $715 million gain on crypto assets held for investment, compared with a $424 million gain in the year-ago quarter.

The exchange's spot trading volume fell 8% quarter over quarter to $135 billion, though the platform continued to gain market share, according to the report.

Notably, Coinbase's prediction markets business surpassed $200 million in annualized revenue, doubling from roughly $100 million in the previous quarter.

"Prediction markets have been one of the fastest-growing products in Coinbase history, growing even through slower sports months driven by crypto contracts where we have a strong right to win," Coinbase CEO Brian Armstrong said in a Thursday statement.

Bernstein analysts wrote in a Friday research note that the growth in prediction markets and subscription and services revenue "helped offset the softer crypto trading activity."

The analysts estimated that prediction markets and derivatives accounted for about 25% of the company's transaction revenue, equivalent to roughly 12% of its total revenue.

Coinbase's derivatives trading volume declined 7% quarter on quarter to roughly $986 billion, while the industry derivatives volumes declined by 11% quarter on quarter, according to Bernstein.

"Subscription and services revenue grew quarter over quarter and now represents nearly half of net revenue. Prediction markets are adding another revenue stream and are less correlated to crypto volatility," said Coinbase CFO Alesia Haas. "These businesses make Coinbase more diversified and resilient across market cycles."

Meanwhile, stablecoin-related revenue remained a key contributor to Coinbase's results. Its stablecoin revenue came in at $328 million, up 3% quarter on quarter. That figure included $21 million attributable to USDC held in corporate balances.

William Blair analysts said Thursday that stablecoins, particularly USDC, will revolutionize cross-border payments and "play a critical role in tokenized RWA settlement."

Both Bernstein and William Blair reiterated their Outperform ratings on Coinbase following the earnings release. Bernstein maintained its $250 price target on Coinbase's stock, while William Blair's valuation analysis suggested the stock could be worth roughly $240.

Coinbase's Nasdaq-listed shares closed down 1.07% at $160.59 on Thursday.

#5125. TRM and Chainalysis Tie $6.4B Binance Hack to North Korea's TraderTraitor

TRM and Chainalysis Tie $6.4B Binance Hack to North Korea's TraderTraitor

Three blockchain analytics firms have attributed this week's hack of Binance to North Korea, with two of the largest naming TraderTraitor, a North Korean hacking group, as responsible for what both describe as the largest crypto theft on record.

TRM Labs said it made the attribution "with high confidence," based on "robust on and off-chain intelligence." It put the theft at about $6.36 billion, moved out of Binance wallets on 10 blockchains, most of it in about 20 minutes from 4:05 p.m. ET on Thursday. That is about four times the $1.5 billion Bybit hack of February 2025, which the FBI attributed to the same group, TRM noted.

Chainalysis, which put the total at about $6.4 billion, said the strongest evidence came when its investigators saw funds stolen from Binance commingling in real time with funds from last month's Bitget exploit, which had been attributed to North Korean actors.

A Chainalysis spokesperson told CNN the firm had "high confidence" North Korea was behind the hack. Singapore-based Pangolin Intelligence also told CNN it believed a North Korean hacking unit was responsible, based on "analyzing the on-chain activity related to this hack and correlating with information gathered from previous known incidents." North Korea has previously denied hacking allegations.

TRM and Chainalysis both said the attacker's operational wallet was funded through Tornado Cash, a crypto mixer. TRM said that and other features, including staging that began well before the theft, matched earlier North Korea-linked attacks such as the Bybit hack and April's KelpDAO exploit.

On Ethereum, the attacker used an EIP-7702 delegation, a feature introduced in Ethereum's Pectra upgrade that lets an ordinary wallet run smart contract code, to turn a Binance hot wallet into a sweeper it controlled, with 50 pre-signed authorizations prepared in advance, according to TRM. Binance wallets on the other nine chains sent funds to the attacker through ordinary signed transfers. In TRM's assessment, that points to a compromise of Binance's hot wallet signing infrastructure.

Binance has said its investigation began with a compromised engineer's laptop, but has since uncovered "additional points of breach." The Bybit attack also began with a single compromised machine: North Korean hackers planted malicious code in the infrastructure of wallet provider Safe after compromising a Safe developer's computer, Decrypt reported at the time.

The attacker has moved quickly to launder what it could not keep. Chainalysis said it is seeing "never-seen-before laundering patterns," with thousands of transactions in a short period suggesting the use of automation to circulate funds repeatedly among addresses. The attacker also sold USDT to market makers and borrowed ETH against USDT on lending protocol Aave to race issuers' freezes, according to TRM, and now holds more than 420,000 ETH and 18,270 BTC, about $2.4 billion beyond any issuer's reach.

Tether, Circle, Ripple and World Liberty Financial have frozen about $2.67 billion in stablecoins, and Arbitrum's Security Council froze 8,426 ETH and 74.6 million ARB, TRM said. Binance puts the total frozen at about $3 billion.

Binance has begun recovering affected assets for users and continues to work with law enforcement, issuers, blockchain intelligence firms and industry partners to trace, freeze and recover additional funds.

The company has already publicly committed to cover losses through… pic.twitter.com/UqtBd37Fpj

In an update on Friday, Binance said it has reserved more than $6.34 billion of company funds to cover the incident, $5 billion of which is already held in the exact tokens that were stolen. It is buying the rest through market and over-the-counter channels, which "can take longer because some tokens may have low liquidity," the exchange said. Those figures do not include frozen assets.

"We will weather this storm," Binance said.

North Korean hackers have long targeted the exchange. In August 2025, Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su told Decrypt that North Korean attackers try to get hired at Binance every day, and called them the single biggest threat to crypto companies. On Wednesday, the U.S. Treasury sanctioned 10 individuals and one entity over North Korean IT worker schemes that moved millions of dollars in crypto.

#5123. Binance says it has $6.34 billion ready to cover hack losses

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Binance already holds $5 billion in affected tokens and is buying the remainder, while industry partners have frozen approximately $3 billion.

Binance has allocated $6.34 billion in its own funds and reserves to cover losses stemming from an earlier security breach, the exchange said in a statement on Friday.

The exchange was hacked on Thursday, with investigations and recovery efforts alongside law enforcement ongoing. It pegged the loss at $6.3 billion.

According to the statement, Binance is holding about $5 billion of the committed funds in the same digital assets that were affected. The exchange did not specify the tokens, but Blockaid, while flagging the incident earlier, said that it was investigating billions in outflows from Binance hot wallets across Ethereum, Arbitrum, and Avalanche.

The exchange is buying the remaining roughly $1.3 billion in tokens through market and over-the-counter channels. However, it cautioned that the process could take longer because of thin liquidity and settlement delays.

Meanwhile, the reserve does not include approximately $3 billion in assets that Binance says industry partners have frozen. Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino separately said the stablecoin giant froze over $1.57 billion in USDT, while Circle confirmed more than $800 million in USDC had been frozen.

Ripple "promptly" froze roughly $200 million in associated funds, co-CEO Yi He said. World Liberty Financial also worked with BitGo and other partners to freeze approximately $27 million in stablecoins and $2 million in its own tokens, according to an X post.

In an earlier statement on Friday, Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said the initial investigation pointed to a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which gave an attacker access to a service container used to submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions.

Teng noted that the incident involved a more complex process than simply exploiting one employee's permissions. Binance has found no evidence of a wallet private key leak.

"Your money is safe, and we're covering every loss," Teng wrote.

The exchange has also suspended withdrawals, but users can still deposit assets, the co-CEO said.

Blockchain intelligence firm TRM Labs said several elements of the attack resemble previous incidents linked to North Korean hackers, including last year's $1.5 billion Bybit breach. Binance has not attributed the incident to a particular group.

TD Cowen said the breach exposed risks in exchange custody and cybersecurity, but did not undermine the underlying utility of digital assets or blockchain networks.

#5122. Texas brothers sentenced to 15 years in prison for armed crypto robbery, kidnapping

Home invasions became the most common form of crypto wrench attacks worldwide during the first half of 2026, rising to 20 incidents from one.

Raymond Christian Garcia and Isiah Angelo Garcia, both of Waller, Texas, were sentenced to 180 months and 186 months in prison, respectively, for their armed robbery of cryptocurrency and kidnapping of a family in Grant, Minnesota.

The brothers were sentenced on Thursday before US District Judge Donovan W. Frank, according to a Friday news release. They had pleaded guilty in June.

On Sept. 19, 2025, the brothers, who had traveled from Texas to Minnesota, kidnapped Victim 1 and his family in their home at gunpoint, court documents said. They zip‑tied the family at gunpoint and held them for more than nine hours, demanding access to Victim 1’s cryptocurrency accounts.

When the brothers did not receive the amount of cryptocurrency expected, they forced Victim 1 to drive to a cabin in upstate Minnesota, where they obtained additional cryptocurrency from other devices for a total of more than $8 million.

The brothers fled after the victim’s son called 911, and police later identified and arrested them near Houston.

“Raymond and Isiah Garcia violated the sanctity of a home, abducted a victim, terrorized a family at gunpoint for hours, and disrupted an entire community in a brazen act of violence and greed,” said FBI Minneapolis Field Office Special Agent in Charge Christopher D. Dotson.

Cointelegraph reported in July that home invasions had become the most common form of crypto wrench attacks during the first half of 2026, rising to 20 publicly reported incidents from just one a year earlier, according to blockchain security firm CertiK.

CertiK said in July it verified 52 wrench attacks worldwide in the first half of 2026, up 33% from 39 incidents during the same period in 2025. Kidnappings rose to 16 from 12.

#5121. Morning Minute: Binance’s $6.4B Hack Overshadows Hyperliquid’s Biggest Day

Morning Minute: Binance’s $6.4B Hack Overshadows Hyperliquid’s Biggest Day

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

Someone stole about $6.4 billion from Binance. That is, by a wide margin, the biggest crypto theft that has ever happened, four times the $1.5 billion taken from Bybit in February 2025.

The money left across three different blockchains: roughly $3.5 billion in ETH, $1.4 billion in BTC, and $630 million on Tron (final accounting still underway).

Luckily, stablecoin issuers can freeze their own tokens; it’s a kill switch built into USDT and USDC. Tether and Circle used it, locking up about $2.39 billion between them. That’s 43% of the haul. That leaves somewhere between $2.6 and $3.2 billion still out there.

Aave also stepped in, freezing its V3 and V4 markets on Ethereum. That’s precautionary, given the worry is an attacker using stolen money to borrow against and leave the protocol holding bad debt.

As this was playing out, Binance stated it’s “aware of reports of a potential security issue” and that user funds are “completely safe,” and paused withdrawals on multiple networks. The clearest comment came from CZ, who no longer runs Binance but is its largest shareholder: it “does look like a hack,” and he expects user funds to be “fully covered.” Later in the evening, Binance announced that it has reserved over $6.34B in company funds to cover losses. So funds are indeed “safu.”

ZachXBT shared that the Binance hack funds were commingled with funds from the $388M Bitget exploit, leading him to believe it’s the same hacker group out of North Korea behind the attack. As for how it happened, Binance pointed to a compromised DevOps engineer’s laptop which was compromised, allowing the attacks to submit the unauthorized transactions, before identifying "additional points of breach" in the following hours.

Markets did what you’d expect. Bitcoin fell below $797,000, ETH fell sub-$2,200 and BNB sub-$700. Over $1.33B in positions got liquidated on the day.

Unfortunately, this came after what was shaping up to be one of the most positive days for a crypto project in its history.

Earlier on Thursday, Trade.xyz announced that Intercontinental Exchange (the company that owns the New York Stock Exchange) is investing in it. The plan is a regulated U.S. venue offering round-the-clock perpetual futures built on Hyperliquid: stocks, indices, commodities, currencies and crypto, with its own exchange and clearing setup, pending CFTC approval.

Stanley Druckenmiller is joining as well, the first of a small group of outside market figures. He already owns the trade; his family office disclosed a $23 million position in Hyperliquid Strategies earlier this year.

So Hyperliquid has its path to a fully regulated perps offering inside the U.S., claims Stanley Druckenmiller as an investor (and a tout on X), and ICE’s CEO said that Hyperliquid was “bigger than NASDAQ.” Doesn’t get much better than that.

I am excited to be an investor in trade(xyz) as they partner with ICE to bring 24/7 perpetual futures on Hyperliquid to the United States. As a market participant, I am looking forward to to using their innovative product to enhance my investment process.

— Stan Druckenmiller (@standuquesne) October 29, 2026

HYPE shot as high as $97 on the day, up more than 20% at peak, before falling to $89 in the Binance hack aftermath. As of this morning, it’s at $93 and still up 8% on the day.

Unfortunately, now we have to deal with the Binance hack aftermath. Hyperliquid is a likely winner as a primary DEX option vs CEXs like Binance (which will likely be less trusted now.) But there’s also a good chance of the entire market being dragged down by fears of more exploits looming.

We will find out soon enough. Expect a bumpy ride…

#5118. Strategy’s preferred-stock fix sets up renewed bitcoin buying, analysts say

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

MSTR fell after third-quarter results, but StoneX and TD Cowen kept buy ratings, citing a $4.83 billion cash reserve and a steadier STRC preferred.

Strategy (MSTR) fell nearly 10% on Friday following its third-quarter earnings, but Wall Street remains bullish on the leading bitcoin treasury company.

Analysts say Strategy has successfully strengthened its balance sheet and stabilized its preferred-stock financing structure, although the effort came at the expense of near-term bitcoin-per-share growth.

Strategy ended the third quarter with $5.07 billion in dollar assets, including a $4.83 billion cash reserve that covers roughly 3.2 to 3.4 years of preferred dividends and debt interest, according to analyst reports from StoneX and TD Cowen.

The company also spent roughly $2 billion repurchasing its STRC preferred stock, helping restore the shares to near their $100 par value and demonstrating greater resilience in its digital credit financing model. However, Strategy's bitcoin per share declined 3.7% year to date as the company prioritized building cash reserves and supporting its preferred securities over accumulating bitcoin.

Separately, shareholders of Strategy's STRC, STRF, STRK and STRD preferred shares approved a change this week allowing dividends to accrue daily, effective Nov. 1 for STRC and Jan. 1 for the other securities.

"Management said the change was intended to reduce volatility, improve liquidity, broaden demand, and move the instruments in the direction of the tokenized, around-the-clock market that they are anticipating," StoneX analyst Mark Palmer wrote.

﻿Expand Chart

With its preferred-stock financing structure more stable and liquidity substantially improved, analysts believe Strategy is better positioned to resume bitcoin accumulation and grow its bitcoin per share.

"We view these developments as important primarily because deeper distribution and better liquidity could ultimately increase Strategy’s capacity to issue Digital Credit efficiently," TD Cowen analysts wrote. "That, rather than STRC's trading price in isolation, remains the connection to MSTR common-equity holders."

StoneX reiterated its buy rating and $435 price target on MSTR, while TD Cowen maintained its buy rating with a $260 price target.

MSTR traded down 8.7% to $129.90 per share on Friday morning. The stock is down approximately 27% year to date.

#5117. Israel Extradites Alleged Nomad Bridge Exploiter to US Over $2.8M Theft: Report

Israel Extradites Alleged Nomad Bridge Exploiter to US Over $2.8M Theft: Report

Israel has extradited an Israeli-Russian citizen to the United States to face charges over the alleged theft of about $2.8 million from crypto bridge Nomad, Israel's State Attorney's Office announced on Thursday, according to a report in The Jerusalem Post.

Alexander Block, formerly known as Alexander Gurevich, was transferred to U.S. authorities early Thursday after unsuccessful appeals in Israel. He was arrested at Ben Gurion Airport in May 2025 while preparing to fly to Russia, shortly after changing his surname and obtaining a passport in his new name.

A federal grand jury in the Northern District of California indicted Block in August 2023 on four counts of computer fraud, one count of transporting stolen property and three counts of money laundering, according to Israel's Supreme Court judgment in the case. The U.S. requested his extradition in December 2024.

According to the U.S. extradition request, as set out in the judgment, a June 2022 software update left a flaw in Nomad's smart contracts that let users receive tokens on one blockchain without locking up the equivalent assets on another. Block, operating under the username "Bitlik," allegedly made six test transfers before carrying out four main transfers around August 1, 2022, receiving tokens worth more than $2.8 million. He then allegedly moved the tokens through several wallets and converted them into other cryptocurrencies to conceal their origin.

His actions exposed the flaw to other Nomad users, who drained a Nomad-controlled wallet holding more than $186 million in user funds, the request alleges. A few days later, Block returned some of the tokens and contacted a senior Nomad official on Telegram, offering to return the rest in exchange for payment for finding the flaw. He later cut off contact, and Nomad went to U.S. law enforcement.

The Jerusalem District Court approved Block's extradition in July 2025. On appeal, he argued that the transactions used functions available within Nomad's automated system, and that the public record of blockchain transfers undermined the money laundering allegations.

The Supreme Court rejected both arguments, holding that exploiting a programming flaw could constitute unlawful computer intrusion even where the software technically permitted the action, The Jerusalem Post reported. It also found that publicly visible transactions do not necessarily identify the people behind wallets or rule out efforts to conceal where funds came from.

Supreme Court President Isaac Amit later rejected Block's request for a further hearing, according to the State Attorney's Office.

The August 2022 exploit drained Nomad of nearly all its funds. Because it took little technical knowledge to copy the original attack, Paradigm researcher Sam Sun likened what followed to "a frenzied free-for-all," Decrypt reported at the time.

In December 2025, the Federal Trade Commission proposed a settlement with Illusory Systems, Nomad's operator, saying the bridge lost $186 million after hackers exploited a poorly tested software update. The settlement would require the company to return recovered funds to users who have not been reimbursed. Nomad recovered about $22 million in the days after the hack.

#5114. Have a Tough Time with Rejection? It Could Be Because of This Common Condition

Whether it’s getting ghosted after a date or realizing you weren’t invited to a friend’s party, rejection stings. But for some people, it hits way harder: sudden anger, tears, shame, or all-consuming emotional pain that feels impossible to get over.

Those people aren’t just “too sensitive” or “overly dramatic.” There’s a real term for what they’re experiencing: Rejection sensitive dysphoria (RSD). While RSD isn’t an official clinical diagnosis, that doesn’t make it any less real for people who live it, according to research in PLOS One.

RSD is often linked to attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), but just about anyone can be affected by it. Ahead, psychology experts explain the science behind RSD, how to spot it, and how to cope with rejection.

Meet the experts: Sasha Hamdani, MD, is a psychiatrist, ADHD clinical specialist, and author of Too Sensitive: Rejection, Resilience, and the Science of Feeling Deeply. Janice Castro, PhD, is a California-based psychologist and founder of J.C. Psychological Services.

“RSD describes an intense emotional response to real or perceived rejection, criticism, or disappointment,” says Sasha Hamdani, MD, a psychiatrist, ADHD clinical specialist, and author of Too Sensitive: Rejection, Resilience, and the Science of Feeling Deeply. The reaction is often much bigger than the event that caused it. “A lot of people can recognize that their reaction is ‘disproportionate’ but still struggle to control its intensity,” Dr. Hamdani says.

When you have RSD, even a mild interaction like a friend not responding to a text can trigger overwhelming feelings of shame, sadness, anger, or inadequacy, Dr. Hamdani says. Some people may feel hurt if their friend didn’t respond to a text, while others may not even think about it. A person with RSD might feel all-consuming emotional pain or distress that seems unbearable as they wait for a reply.

RSD isn’t recognized in the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM-5), the official manual psychologists use to diagnose people with mental health conditions. That just means that it’s not an official diagnosis—but experts still find it important to recognize and understand. Janice Castro, PhD, a California-based psychologist and founder of J.C. Psychological Services, has seen RSD in her own work with clients—especially those with ADHD—and says that more therapists are starting to use the term. “Just because science hasn’t caught up yet doesn’t mean the experience isn’t real,” she says.

The RSD conversation is important because it gives many people a name for an experience they previously didn’t have words for, Castro says. The more therapists and the general public talk about RSD, the closer we get to having more tools and strategies people can use to navigate their experience. “For those of us who have loved ones that experience RSD, we can also start having open conversations about how to support them—and maybe have a little more empathy rather than judge or criticize.”

You can thank evolution. “If you think back to the Stone Age, for example, people were just trying to survive in community, and the idea of not having social connection or social bonds was a threat to survival,” says Castro. Nowadays, your body can react to rejection on a visceral level, as if you were in danger, and the surge of stress hormones can send you into fight-or-flight mode.

From a neuroscience perspective, rejection can overwhelm the brain. The amygdala (which helps detect potential threats) activates a stress response, while the prefrontal cortex (responsible for emotional regulation) may have difficulty putting the situation into context, Dr. Hamdani explains. “Essentially, the emotional response can happen faster than the brain can logically navigate,” she says.

Still, it’s unclear what exactly causes RSD. But if you deal with anxiety, depression, or naturally engage in a lot of self-criticism, you may be more likely to experience it, says Castro. Genetics, environmental context, relationships, and family history are important considerations, too, she says.

Anecdotally, RSD is often associated with neurodivergence—which can include ADHD, autism spectrum disorder, dyslexia, and dyspraxia, to name a few—but more research is needed to understand how common it is.

RSD is most often talked about in relation to ADHD. People with ADHD can feel things intensely and quickly, and react impulsively, says Castro. If they feel rejected or excluded while already struggling to regulate their emotions, they may react in a way that’s disproportionate to what happened, she explains. This is one reason RSD is so often linked to ADHD. However, RSD isn’t exclusive to ADHD, Dr. Hamdani says. “People with anxiety, trauma histories, or other mental health conditions can also experience similar patterns.”

One example is social anxiety disorder. “There’s often an intense fear that you’re going to be rejected—or that something you say or do is going to be extremely embarrassing,” Castro says. You might avoid certain people or settings to evade potential discomfort. RSD is also common for people with borderline personality disorder (BPD) since it can involve deep fear of abandonment and criticism, Castro says.

You also might be more sensitive to rejection if you have obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), especially if you tend to ruminate on social situations. It can be an exhausting cycle: “You’re constantly thinking about a conversation, what you said, how it landed, then criticizing yourself and seeking reassurance,” Castro says.

Plus, people with a history of trauma can be extremely aware of social cues and, as a result, rejection. They might constantly wonder if others approve of them or fear upsetting others, Castro says. If they’ve had a traumatic experience, their nervous system is likely heightened, making them more sensitive to negative things happening again, she explains.

Read on for symptoms of RSD, per Dr. Hamdani. Remember: Anyone can experience a combination of any of the below emotions after being rejected (it stings, no matter what!). But, if you’re feeling these consistently and intensely, that could be RSD.

RSD looks different for everyone, and coping skills will depend on the individual. That said, here are a few recommendations for navigating RSD, whether you have ADHD or not, per experts:

Dr. Hamdani recommends creating space between the rejection (or feeling of rejection) and your response. “This might mean taking a few slow breaths, physically stepping away from the situation, or asking yourself, ‘What do I actually know happened, and what am I assuming happened?’” she says.

To help with this, Castro recommends 4-7-8 breathing. “Breathe in through your nose for four seconds, hold your breath for seven seconds, then release through your mouth for eight seconds,” she says. Pretend you’re exhaling through a straw so the air comes out slowly. “It's not going to reduce the pain, and it's not going to make the rejection go away—but it will reduce the intensity of your emotion so that you can pause before reacting,” Castro says.

Self-compassion, the practice of being supportive toward yourself when you’re experiencing challenges, is an important part of coping with RSD. This is especially true if you have ADHD and were diagnosed in adulthood, since you’ve likely “faced ongoing judgment, criticism, and reprimanding for making mistakes [due to ADHD]” over the years, Castro says. “That’s decades of hearing messages that you aren’t good enough, which becomes internalized negative self-talk and self-imposed pressure,” she explains.

If you’re going through RSD, Castro recommends trying a self-compassion exercise from Kristin Neff, PhD: Ask yourself, How would you respond to a friend in this situation? “As humans, we are our own worst critics, yet the way we talk to our friends is often with kindness,” Castro says. “Why not try treating yourself like a good friend and see what happens?”

Whether you have ADHD or not, taking care of yourself is crucial—that way if and when RSD hits, you at least have a good foundation for well-being, which will help you cope more effectively. Dr. Castro recommends prioritizing sleep (around seven to nine hours, ideally), balanced nutrition, and hydration to help regulate your mood.

Stress and anxiety can make RSD even worse, per Cleveland Clinic, so it’s important to manage them effectively. If you have ADHD, setting reminders for yourself can be an important part of stress management. “People with ADHD put in a lot of work every day to keep up with structure and routines that most neurotypical-functioning adults perceive as ‘easy,’ or that neurotypical folks ‘just do,’” Castro says. Little reminders can help you feel less overwhelmed even if you don’t have ADHD, so don’t be afraid to put those alarm settings to good use.

Both cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) and dialectical behavior therapy (DBT) can be helpful for RSD, Dr. Hamdani says. Working with a licensed therapist can help you “recognize rejection-related thought patterns, challenge your assumptions, and develop healthier emotional responses,” she says.

If RSD is causing you to avoid social outings or work meetings, or is otherwise affecting daily life, it’s time to talk with a professional, Castro says. Role-playing is one of her favorite tools since it lets clients practice how they want to respond in future scenarios.

If you struggle with ADHD or any of the mental health conditions mentioned earlier—or if it feels like RSD is taking over your life—consider asking your doctor or psychiatrist if medication would be a good option. For people with ADHD, various ADHD medications can help with broader emotional regulation, but no medication is specifically FDA-approved for RSD and the research is limited, Dr. Hamdani says.

Rejection is never fun, and it’s normal to feel hurt. But if you’ve been told you’re “too sensitive,” know that your emotions are valid and there’s support out there. And to anyone who says you need to tone down your feelings, Dr. Hamdani would like a word: “The goal isn’t to become less sensitive, but to feel more in control.”

#5112. What is EIP-7702? The Ethereum Upgrade Behind the $6.4B Binance Hack

What is EIP-7702? The Ethereum Upgrade Behind the $6.4B Binance Hack

Ethereum has two kinds of accounts. Externally owned accounts (EOAs), the standard wallets most people use, are controlled by a private key and can do little more than send transactions. Smart contract accounts can run code, enabling features like spending limits, account recovery and paying fees in tokens other than ETH, but most users never moved to them.

EIP-7702 is an attempt to bridge that gap. Instead of asking users to migrate to new wallets, it lets existing EOAs borrow the code of a smart contract while keeping their address and funds.

This guide explains what EIP-7702 is, how it works, why developers wanted it, and how attackers used it in the largest crypto theft on record.

EIP-7702, titled "Set Code for EOAs," is an Ethereum Improvement Proposal written in May 2024 by Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin and developers Sam Wilson, Ansgar Dietrichs and lightclient, according to the official specification.

It was one of the headline changes in Pectra, a major Ethereum upgrade that went live on May 7, 2025.

"EIP-7702 lays the foundation we've needed for a long time," Ethereum core developer Preston Van Loon told Decrypt at the time. "While full abstraction won't be immediately available to end users with Pectra, this upgrade gives Ethereum the infrastructure to support it moving forward."

EIP-7702 is a change to Ethereum's core protocol, not a token or a separate blockchain, so there is nothing to buy.

EIP-7702 adds a new type of Ethereum transaction, the "set code transaction." It carries a list of authorizations, each signed with the private key of the account being upgraded, that names the address of a smart contract the account wants to use.

Once the transaction is processed, the account stores a small pointer, called a delegation indicator, to that contract. From then on, whenever anyone calls the account, Ethereum runs the contract's code as if it belonged to the account, with full access to its funds.

The delegation lasts until the account owner signs a new authorization, either pointing to a different contract or clearing the pointer to restore a plain EOA.

EIP-7702 marks delegated accounts using the byte 0xef, a code value that was banned in earlier upgrades so it could be reserved for special purposes like this.

The specification names three main features it was designed to deliver:

Those same features carry risks. Because delegated code gets "unrestricted access to the account," apps should never ask users to sign an EIP-7702 authorization, and wallets should not offer an interface for it, the specification warns. "There is no safe way to provide this interface," it says.

Scammers moved quickly. Within weeks of Pectra's launch, trading firm Wintermute found that more than 80% of EIP-7702 delegations pointed to copies of the same malicious "sweeper" contract, which automatically drains wallets whose keys have leaked, The Block reported in June 2025.

On October 29, 2026, an attacker moved some $6.4 billion out of Binance wallets on 10 blockchains, most of it within about 20 minutes. Blockchain analytics firms TRM Labs and Chainalysis both attributed the theft to TraderTraitor, the North Korean hacking group behind the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025.

On Ethereum, the attacker used an EIP-7702 delegation to turn a Binance hot wallet into a sweeper it controlled, with 50 authorizations signed in advance, according to TRM Labs. An operational wallet funded through crypto mixer Tornado Cash submitted the signed authorizations, which let the hot wallet run attacker-deployed code, and the attacker then called the wallet to sweep its assets into addresses it controlled, Chainalysis said.

Crucially, EIP-7702 delegations require a valid signature from the account being upgraded. Binance wallets on the other nine chains also sent funds to the attacker through ordinary signed transfers, which TRM said points to a compromise of Binance's hot wallet signing infrastructure rather than a flaw in Ethereum itself.

Binance has said its investigation began with a compromised engineer's laptop, and has since found "additional points of breach."

EIP-7702 is a first step toward full account abstraction, a long-standing goal of making every Ethereum account as flexible as a smart contract. Its authors designed it to be forward-compatible with that "endgame," so wallets built on it today should keep working as Ethereum's account model evolves.

The next step could come with Hegotá, the upgrade planned to follow Glamsterdam on Ethereum's roadmap. In February 2026, Buterin said that EIP-8141, which he called "an omnibus that wraps up and solves every remaining problem" account abstraction was meant to address, looked "possible to make happen within a year" in the Hegotá fork.

We have been talking about account abstraction ever since early 2016, see the original EIP-86: https://t.co/HYLSTLHgWH

Now, we finally have EIP-8141 ( https://t.co/jYqeS55j6P ), an omnibus that wraps up and solves every remaining problem that AA was…

— vitalik.eth (@VitalikButerin) February 28, 2026

"We have been talking about account abstraction ever since early 2016," Buterin wrote, adding that developers are also discussing whether existing accounts could join the new framework, which would give them batching and sponsored transactions natively.

It is part of a wider rebuild. In July 2026, Buterin described a new draft of Ethereum's long-term roadmap as the network's biggest overhaul since the Merge, its 2022 switch to proof of stake.

#5110. Bitcoin turns 18: Has the original rebel grown up?

In typical Bitcoin style, the number one cryptocurrency turns 18 today, amid AI-driven security threats, quantum fears and the biggest exchange hack in crypto history.

Eighteen years ago today, pseudonymous cypherpunk Satoshi Nakamoto posted the Bitcoin whitepaper, Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System, to a cryptography mailing list.

Created to bypass opaque institutions and remove the need for trust in financial transactions, Bitcoin (BTC) has been taking on the traditional financial system since its genesis block was mined on Jan. 3, 2009.

Inscribed in that very first block was a message from Satoshi, quoting a headline from The Times: “Chancellor on brink of second bailout for banks.” It captured the financial crisis backdrop against which Bitcoin was born.

From its early years to adulthood, Bitcoin has had a tumultuous journey, fraught with controversy, intrigue and downright skulduggery.

As challenging as the journey has been, Bitcoin’s future remains on fragile ground, with the fallout from the $6.3 billion Binance hack, the threat of quantum computing, the acceleration of AI-related cyber threats, and questions over its long-term security still looming.

As Bitcoin celebrates its 18th birthday, it’s now old enough to vote in the US midterm elections, but is it ready for adulthood?

Undoubtedly, one of Bitcoin’s most memorable moments was on May 22, 2010 when developer Laszlo Hanyecz sent 10,000 BTC to pay for two pizzas in one of the earliest documented Bitcoin transactions.

The Bitcoin Whitepaper. Source: Bitcoin.org

The day would become known as Bitcoin Pizza Day and go down in crypto folklore as the most expensive pizza purchase on record. The coins were worth around $41 at the time, taking the concept of opportunity cost to a whole new high — those pizzas would have cost $780 million at today’s prices.

Related: 10 of the greatest unsolved crypto mysteries

Bitcoin traded for pennies in its earliest days, but Satoshi understood how that would change one day, writing in 2009, “It might make sense just to get some in case it catches on.”

By late 2013, Bitcoin had broken through the $1,000 milestone for the first time, amid growing public interest with the emergence of the Silk Road darknet marketplace.

Yet nowhere do the laws of physics apply more pertinently than crypto; inevitably, what goes up must come down. In February 2014, Bitcoin’s epic run came to an abrupt end when Mt. Gox, the world’s largest Bitcoin exchange at the time, suddenly stopped trading and went offline.

The exchange had lost around 850,000 BTC, including customers’ funds, in a collapse that sent shockwaves through the industry, making Mt. Gox an early poster child for the concept “not your keys, not your coins.”

The next dramatic bull run would culminate in Bitcoin’s price climbing from below $1,000 at the beginning of 2017 to almost $20,000 by December.

The rally coincided with a boom in initial coin offerings (ICOs) and a rush of projects promising to use blockchain technology for everything from banking to supply chains, and even Long Island iced tea.

Suddenly, every man, woman and dog wanted a blockchain, even if they had no idea how Bitcoin worked or where it came from.

Everyone, that is, except JPMorgan Chase CEO Jamie Dimon, who called Bitcoin “a fraud” in September 2017 and said he would fire any employee trading it.

Bitcoin genesis block. Source: Mempool.space

At the time, Bitcoin was embroiled in a bitter debate over how the network should scale. The so-called “block size war” peaked in August 2017, culminating in the hard fork that created Bitcoin Cash and highlighting the complications of trying to change a decentralized system.

Related: Private, fast USDT is coming to Bitcoin. Can it compete with Ethereum and Tron?

Meanwhile, China moved against the industry, banning ICOs in September 2017, taking action against cryptocurrency exchanges and fanning the market uncertainty ahead of the eventual downturn in 2018.

Speculation over the identity of Bitcoin’s creator had also become a sideshow, fueled by Australian computer scientist Craig Wright’s repeated claims to be Satoshi Nakamoto. The UK High Court rejected his claim in 2024.

Bitcoin’s next major bull run came during the COVID-19 pandemic, as governments and central banks unleashed a tsunami of money printing in a bid to cushion economies from lockdowns.

Interest rates fell, liquidity flooded financial markets and Bitcoin, which had plunged during the March 2020 market panic, began a remarkable recovery, reaching almost $69,000 by November 2021.

As if China’s intervention wasn’t enough the first time around, Beijing stepped in again to rain on the parade, announcing a sweeping ban on cryptocurrency transactions and mining in September 2021, and Bitcoin’s price took a hit.

In November 2022, another major cryptocurrency exchange, FTX, collapsed, intensifying the crypto winter and leaving customers facing enormous losses.

The episode led to a loss of trust from the TradFi sector and a spate of regulatory hostility toward crypto, in what the industry would dub “Operation Chokepoint 2.0.” Bitcoin’s downward spiral was once again painful and steep.

Just when sentiment in the industry was circling the drain, a silver lining appeared in January 2024 from one of the unlikeliest sources. The US Securities and Exchange Commission, which had repeatedly rejected spot Bitcoin product applications, approved the first US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded products.

The SEC approved a US spot Bitcoin ETF. Source: SEC.gov

The nod from the SEC finally gave investors a regulated route to gain exposure to BTC without having to manage wallets or private keys themselves. At the end of that year, Bitcoin’s price broke through $100,000 for the first time.

Related: Bitcoin ETF inflows climb to $176M as BTC rebounds above $80K

Since then, traditional finance has continued to move closer to crypto, with banks moving into custody and trading services and heavyweights like BlackRock CEO Larry Fink talking about putting everything from stocks to bonds on the blockchain.

Even Dimon shifted his stance from outright dismissal to allowing clients to buy the asset. The original cryptocurrency went on to hit a new all-time high of more than $126,000 in October 2025.

Bitcoin is firmly in the embrace of TradFi once again, with some sizable regulatory tailwinds from Washington and abroad.

The Guiding and Establishing National Innovation in US Stablecoins (GENIUS) Act has established a federal framework for stablecoins, the SEC has offered greater clarity on how securities laws apply to crypto assets, and Europe’s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA) has given the local industry a more harmonized regulatory framework.

Yet the institutionalization of the space has led many Bitcoiners to question whether institutions holding so much BTC are concentrating ownership and centralizing supply — and indeed whether the cypherpunk movement has been captured by the very institutions it sought to rebel against.

There are bigger questions looming over Bitcoin’s long-term security, too, including the threat of quantum computing, which many fear could break the cryptography that secures Bitcoin.

A proposed upgrade known as BIP-360 outlines a new type of Bitcoin output to reduce certain quantum vulnerabilities, but as the community learned from the block size war, the challenge will be agreeing on any new implementation before the threat arrives.

And quantum computing is not the only security question Bitcoin needs to resolve. This week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, the largest crypto exchange hack on record, has renewed fears about the security of centralized exchanges, the stability of crypto infrastructure and the potential for further market turmoil.

There’s also the question of who pays to keep the network secure as the block subsidy trends to zero, and whether transaction fees alone will be enough to compensate the miners securing the network.

Transaction fees accounted for just 0.67% of total miner revenue in the 90 days to Oct. 4 and have remained below 5% of miner revenue for most of Bitcoin’s history. That leaves a huge gap for fees to fill as block rewards diminish with each halving.

Bitcoin has endured more than your average teen as it comes of age, surviving exchange collapses, regulatory crackdowns, civil wars and repeated market crashes.

It has also become something Satoshi may not have foreseen: an asset held by Wall Street and traded through mainstream financial products.

Magazine: Will gold’s ‘infinite mint bug’ make Bitcoin more valuable?

#5109. President Trump says they’re looking into Binance hack as calls grow for crypto rules

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, The Digital Chamber CEO Cody Carbone sent a letter to House and Senate leadership, demanding that Congress immediately pass a bill that would give the CFTC authority over crypto exchanges.

Following the $6.3 billion hack of the world's largest crypto exchange, Binance, calls are growing for legislation and for firms like Binance to move onshore.

On Thursday, Binance suffered that blow following what its co-CEO Richard Teng said was caused in part by a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop that gave an attacker access to a service container used to submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions. Blockchain analytics firms like TRM Labs have attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a hacking unit in North Korea.

During a press conference on Friday afternoon, President Donald Trump said he was looking into the hack, adding that Binance has said they are ensuring customers get their funds back.

"But it's a hack," he said. "That's the problem. You know the old-fashioned way when you used to sign a check… these are different people, never see the money anymore. So I've always felt it was a little bit dangerous."

Earlier on CNBC, Commodity Futures Trading Commission Chair Michael Selig pointed to Binance not being federally regulated by his agency as an issue. On Oct. 5, the agency proposed its federal framework for crypto, specifically for leveraged and margined retail crypto trading and a new registration category for exchanges offering those products.

"This proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Selig said. "The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States."

Both Republicans and Democrats have expressed concerns about Binance over the past few years, particularly when it came to money laundering and its consumer protections. In 2023, the exchange's previous CEO Changpeng Zhao pleaded guilty to failing to implement adequate anti-money-laundering controls at Binance as part of a sweeping $4 billion settlement. He was later pardoned by Trump.

Whether or not the Clarity Act, broad crypto legislation to regulate exchanges along with other facets of the industry, would have helped freeze the transactions lost in the Binance hack also came up. The bill was passed by the House last year, but has since stalled in the Senate amid concerns over Trump's vast crypto wealth. That wealth had grown to hundreds of millions of dollars linked to World Liberty Financial and his TRUMP memecoin and became something many Democrats could not ignore in trying to pass the bill.

"Yesterday, hackers stole more than $6 billion from Binance in less than 4 hours, the biggest heist in history," said one of the bill's negotiators, Sen. Cynthia Lummis, R-Wyo., in a post on X. "The Clarity Act would have given crypto firms legal cover to freeze suspicious transactions the instant law enforcement calls. Every minute lost is money gone for good."

Amanda Fischer, policy director and chief operating officer for Better Markets and who was the previous chief of staff at the Securities and Exchange Commission, pushed back, saying that the bill would have required stablecoin issuers to "freeze per a court order," adding that at the same time crypto firms worked as quickly as they could to freeze the assets, in a post on X.

However, Solana Policy Institute General Counsel Patrick Wilson said that wasn't right and that a section in the Clarity Act "expressly protected stablecoin issuers from private lawsuits for voluntary, temporary holds based on a reasonable belief of a violation of law. No court order required."

On Friday, The Digital Chamber CEO Cody Carbone sent a letter to House and Senate leadership, demanding that Congress immediately pass a bill that would give the CFTC authority over crypto exchanges and keep customer funds and company funds separate from each other in bankruptcy proceedings.

"No federal regulator can set and enforce consistent standards for how these platforms hold, segregate, and secure customer assets, or for their cybersecurity, before consumers are harmed," Carbone said. "Regulators can only act after the damage is done."

"We urge you to act before this Congress adjourns," he added.

Rep. Tom Timmons, R-S.C., meanwhile, brought up national security concerns.

"America cannot allow rogue regimes to exploit digital assets to finance their nuclear weapons and ballistic missile programs," Timmons said in a post on X.

#5107. 7 Myths You’ve Heard About Creatine That Aren’t Actually True, According to Experts

Don’t believe everything you’ve heard about the supp.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

If there’s one supplement on everyone’s lips, it’s creatine. But, with all the talk about this well-studied supp that was once for gym bros and is now beloved by, well, everyone, it can be hard to separate fact from fiction.

Creatine is a compound your body makes naturally from three amino acids called arginine, glycine, and methionine, but you can also get small amounts from meat and fish, says Kristin Kirkpatrick, RDN, a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness and Preventive Medicine. “I like to describe it as the power plant for your cells,” she says. “It helps regenerate adenosine triphosphate (ATP), the energy molecule your muscles and brain rely on for quick, high-intensity work.” Without it, your movements won’t be as explosive in the gym, and you may take longer to recover, as Women’s Health reported previously.

The body produces about half of the creatine it needs, and the rest has to come from diet—or supplements. But as creatine’s popularity has exploded, so has the misinformation surrounding it. We asked experts to debunk some of the most persistent myths about the supplement. Here’s what they said.

Meet the experts: Jay Luthar, MD, is an internal, integrative, and lifestyle medicine physician and a professor at Harvard Medical School. Kristin Kirkpatrick, RDN, is a dietitian at Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness and Preventive Medicine.

You may have heard that when you first start taking creatine, you should do what’s called a loading phase—which is taking about four times the regular dose for up to a week—to saturate your muscle stores with the supp.

A standard dose of creatine is 3 to 5 grams per day; a loading phase calls for about 20 grams per day, split into four doses, Kirkpatrick explains. Why four doses? “Essentially, there’s a ceiling on how much creatine the gut can absorb and deliver to muscle at any one time,” says Jay Luthar, MD, an internal, integrative, and lifestyle medicine physician and professor at Harvard Medical School. “Taking 20 grams at once doesn’t mean 20 grams reaches your muscles; it means a portion gets excreted, and you likely experience GI discomfort in the process.”

The idea is that a loading phase fills your muscles' creatine stores faster—in about a week—but it’s totally unnecessary for most people. An athlete who wants results before an upcoming competition may benefit, for example, but if you don’t have anything timely on the calendar, you can skip the load completely, Kirkpatrick says. Doing so might also save you from bloating and stomach upset and other side effects that most often cause people to stop taking the supp. “A steady daily dose is simpler, easier on your body, and just as effective over time,” Kirkpatrick says.

This one is partly true—but stay with us. Creatine works by drawing more water into your muscles, which means that it’s possible you may experience some bloat at first. Key words: At first. Research in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition from 2021 found that creatine does not cause long-term water retention.

“In the initial few weeks, creatine draws water into muscle cells,” Dr. Luthar says. “That means the bump on the scale is evident, but it’s water weight—not fat. Once muscle saturation is reached and you transition to a maintenance dose, this stabilizes.” As for how long that takes? About two to four weeks, he says.

Also keep in mind that your dosage and creatine type matter. “The bloating and stomach discomfort people complain about are usually tied to large doses of creatine monohydrate, especially during a loading phase,” says Kirkpatrick. “Creatine monohydrate isn't very soluble, so large amounts can sit in the gut and cause GI distress.” If this is a concern, Kirkpatrick recommends creatine HCl, which is much more soluble and better absorbed, so it works at a smaller dose. That means less bloating, less stomach upset, and less water retention, with the same benefits creatine is known for.

For years, creatine was relegated to lifters, but now there is research showing benefits far beyond the gym. “We now have strong evidence that creatine supports healthy aging and muscle preservation during weight loss,” says Kirkpatrick.

For example, a new study in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition found that adults ages 45 to 65 gained lean tissue and strength on creatine without a structured exercise program. “That's why I see interest [in creatine] from people who have never lifted a weight: midlife women, older adults, people on GLP-1 medications, and anyone thinking about longevity,” says Kirkpatrick.

Plus, endurance athletes can see some perks from the supplement too. Emphasis on some. Although creatine doesn't appear to improve aerobic capacity, meaning it won't make you a faster marathoner on its own, endurance athletes can still see benefits when their sport includes explosive movements like sprints, surges, or hill climbs, as well as in the strength training they do to support injury prevention, per a 2023 systematic review and meta-analysis in Sports Medicine.

“Creatine’s primary mechanism is supporting short-burst, high-intensity energy production, which isn’t the primary energy system in steady-state endurance events,” Dr. Luthar says. “That can make it useful if a marathon runner, for example, is doing weekly sprint workouts to improve speed. It might have less of an impact on a weekly long run or three-hour cycle ride.” There’s also interesting emerging evidence that creatine helps reduce recovery time between workouts, shares Dr. Luthar. For endurance athletes and lifters alike, this could be another potential benefit.

Some of the most exciting creatine research is happening outside the weight room, especially for women. First, a few facts to keep in mind, per Kirkpatrick: Women naturally store less creatine than men, and beginning around age 30, we lose 3 to 8 percent of our muscle mass every decade, with the loss accelerating after 50. The good news is that creatine supplementation has been shown to support strength, muscle, and recovery in women at every life stage, particularly when paired with resistance training, according to a 2021 study published in Nutrients.

Build muscle with the WH+ Strength After 50 Plan.

Another emerging area of creatine research in women deals with menopause. Perimenopausal women have measurably lower brain creatine levels, and those deficits are linked to difficulty concentrating, according to recent research in Nutrients. And, although a lot more studies are needed, it’s possible supplementation could help with that. A 2025 randomized controlled trial published in the Journal of the American Nutrition Association found that eight weeks of low-dose creatine HCl improved cognitive function and brain energy metabolism in perimenopausal and menopausal women.

Estrogen loss associated with menopause also accelerates muscle loss (which is why women start to see a steeper decline in their 50s), so creatine could potentially help both mentally and physically, as noted by a 2025 study published in the Journal of the Society of Sports Nutrition International. Just keep in mind: Though creatine has benefits for women, that doesn’t mean that we need gender-specific formulations.

Consistency matters far more than the timing of your creatine intake, says Kirkpatrick. “Creatine works by gradually filling and maintaining your muscles' stores, so what counts is taking it every day, including rest days.”

You don’t need to be super exact on when you take it every day, either. Creatine works through muscle saturation maintained over time, so if you miss your usual window by a few hours, there won’t be a huge effect on stores, says Dr. Luthar.

Kirkpatrick’s advice is to stack creatine onto something you already do every day—just maybe not your morning coffee. When mixed with hotter liquids, creatine powder may become ineffective, so it’s best to stick to room temperature or cool liquids.

This myth comes from a bit of a misunderstanding. Creatine gets broken down into creatinine, which is often measured in routine bloodwork as an estimate of kidney function. Elevated levels of creatinine can indicate an issue with kidney function. But, if you’re taking a creatine supplement, you may have elevated levels of creatinine simply because you’re taking in extra amounts—not because your kidneys aren’t functioning well. For that reason, Kirkpatrick says to tell your doctor you take creatine before bloodwork, so a slightly higher creatinine number is interpreted correctly.

When researchers looked into the relationship between creatine and kidney health, they found that creatine did not harm kidney function, per a 2026 meta-analysis of 26 randomized controlled trials published in International Urology and Nephrology.

Overall—for a supplement—creatine is pretty low stakes. “It's one of the most researched supplements available, with a strong safety record,” Kirkpatrick says. A 2025 analysis published in the Journal of the International Society of Sports Nutrition evaluated 685 human clinical trials and found no significant difference in reported side effects between creatine and placebo. That said, you should talk to a doctor before taking any new supplement, especially if you have kidney or liver disease, diabetes, or bipolar disorder, or are pregnant or breastfeeding. (For individuals diagnosed with bipolar disorder, Kirkpatrick says there is some preliminary research suggesting that creatine supplementation may increase the risk of manic or hypomanic episodes.)

You’ll also want to choose a brand that is third-party tested, since supplements aren’t regulated like medications. (You can find our recs here.)

No supplement is a must—and that includes creatine. Kirkpatrick says you can absolutely build strength and muscle without it. “Consistent resistance training, enough protein, adequate sleep, and a well-balanced diet will always do the heavy lifting,” says Kirkpatrick. “Creatine is just the add-on, not the foundation.”

That said, Kirkpatrick thinks it deserves the love it gets, particularly when we look at the growing body of research surrounding its benefits. “What makes creatine particularly exciting is the strong evidence supporting its role in muscle strength, performance, and recovery, along with emerging research exploring potential benefits for brain health and cognitive function,” says Kirkpatrick. “For most healthy adults, creatine is considered safe when taken at recommended doses. While the research is especially compelling for muscle health, I think the potential applications for healthy aging make this an exciting area of nutritional science.”

Dr. Luthar agrees and says that creatine is backed by decades of sports nutrition research, including a fair number of studies with human participants. Still, the supplement has taken social media by storm and the hype is taking on a life of its own. “Some of the benefits posted online are exaggerated or incorrect, which is why it’s best to discuss creatine or any supplement with a health care provider before taking it,” he says.

Ashley Martens, C.S.C.S. is a wellness writer based in Chicago. With a lifelong passion for all things health and wellness, Ashley enjoys writing about topics to help people live happier and healthier lives. With a foundation in fitness, food, and nutrition, Ashley covers it all including sexual health and travel topics. Ashley is also a NASM-certified personal trainer and group fitness instructor.
