#5067. Binance says phase one of withdrawals opens after $6.3B hack

The crypto exchange said it has begun phased withdrawals after the record hack the FBI has blamed on North Korea.

Binance said it has reopened its Bitcoin network in phase one of resuming withdrawals after a major hack of about $6.3 billion in funds.

The crypto exchange had suspended user withdrawals after the attack on Thursday, which the FBI blamed on North Korean operatives.

“First batch network now opened in phase one of withdrawals is $BTC bitcoin network,” Binance said on X.

In an earlier X announcement on Sunday, Binance had said it would roll out withdrawal services in phases, which will be progressively available. Updates will be available on its official X channel, it added.

“We are taking a phased approach by prioritizing tokens based on the past week data of user demand, so that each network and asset pair can be restored carefully, with stability and security checks completed before access is reopened,” Binance said.

Users should check the withdrawal page for their selected asset and network, or visit the Binance network status page to confirm availability, the company said.

During the phased rollout, it is expected that there will be some congestion, longer processing times and even temporary delays, Binance said, so if a withdrawal is not successful, users should try again after a short while.

Cointelegraph reported on Saturday that Binance said it had replenished 90% of the assets stolen in the hack, up from 80% a day earlier, and remained on track to resume withdrawals Sunday.

#5065. The Brooks Running Shoes to Power Your Soccer Training

Find the right pair for sprint work, endurance runs, and active recovery days.

Anyone who’s ever huffed and puffed their way through a pickup soccer game after not playing for a while knows how quickly the sport can expose your conditioning. A match requires repeated bursts of speed with few opportunities to catch your breath, making stamina essential from the first minute through the 90th or beyond.

The average elite soccer player covers about 6.4 miles per game. But even if you’re just a weekend rec-leaguer, cross-training can help you cover more ground without fading late in the match. Running—including sprints, endurance work, and easy jogs—should be a regular part of your routine, and the right shoes can help you get more out of every workout.

Not sure which pair you need? Brooks—a leader in performance running shoes—offers an option for every type of run. Whether you’re doing sprint repeats, heading out for a 10K, or keeping things easy on an off day, here’s how to choose the right model for your workout.

You know what you don’t want to think about when you’re doing sprint drills? Your feet—or at least how your feet feel in your shoes. During workouts built around acceleration and all-out speed, you need a shoe that delivers support, flexibility, and comfort. For the Glycerin Flex, that’s check, check, and check.

Support comes in the form of Brooks’ proprietary nitrogen-infused DNA TUNED midsole foam, which features large cells in the heel to cushion your landings and smaller cells in the forefoot for responsive toe-offs. The combination helps you land comfortably and push off with power as you pick up the pace.

For sprint work, you also need a shoe that moves with you. The Glycerin Flex’s FlexZone technology combines a midfoot flex groove with a podular forefoot design to provide flexibility and support where you need it. A thin layer of sticky rubber on the outsole adds traction as you work on your speed.

You cover plenty of ground every time you take the field for a practice or match, making endurance work essential if you want to keep going through the final whistle. Longer runs can have a place in your conditioning on the days you aren’t wearing your cleats, as long as you build gradually and don’t overdo the mileage.

That’s where the Brooks Ghost Amp comes in. This new take on the brand’s popular Ghost model combines comfort with an energized ride for days when you want to take on a few more miles. Key among its features is SpringRamp forefoot technology, which helps you push off the balls of your feet with a little extra oomph.

Also new is the shoe’s nitrogen-infused DNA HYPE cushioning, which combines two types of foam: lightweight PEBA, which provides high energy return and a springy feel, and EVA, which offers cushioning and stability. Together, they deliver a balance of softness and responsiveness.

Up top, the lightweight warp-knit upper provides breathability and comfort, with enough structure to help secure your foot when you pick up the pace. The eye-catching color options add a little more personality to your training miles.

Recovery days are an essential part of any athlete’s training, especially soccer players. An easy walk or jog can help you stay loose, ease stiffness, and give your mind and body a reset.

But slowing the pace doesn’t mean forgetting about your footwear. On active recovery days, you still want a shoe that provides support and keeps you comfortable. The Glycerin Max 2 does both. Though it’s also built for longer runs, this plush shoe is a natural fit for easier days.

The defining feature of the Glycerin Max 2 is its dramatic GlideRoll Rocker technology. Its sculpted bevel and toe spring help your foot move smoothly from heel to toe. The shoe also features a high stack of DNA TUNED foam, which provides generous cushioning underfoot to keep your legs feeling fresh and protect your body against impact.

Active recovery may not directly put the ball in the net, but the work you do between matches can help you feel ready for the next one. And the right shoe can make that work more comfortable.

Strive has acquired another 124 BTC for approximately $10 million, bringing its total holdings to 43,124 BTC.

Bitcoin treasury company Strive Strive, Inc. (ASST) bought another 124 Bitcoin (BTC) between Oct. 24 and Oct. 30, bringing its total treasury to 43,124 BTC, according to a Monday filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Strive launched the program on Oct. 26, allowing it to sell up to $1 billion of SATS preferred shares to fund additional bitcoin purchases and other corporate expenses.

Strive also continues to hold 505,000 shares of Strategy's STRC preferred stock, valued at approximately $50.1 million as of Oct. 30. Its SATA preferred shares outstanding remained unchanged at just over 14 million.

#5063. DeepSeek developing AI agent requiring less human input: Report

The proposal comes amid intense competition among AI developers, including US companies Anthropic and OpenAI, the SCMP said.

Chinese artificial intelligence company DeepSeek has revealed a design to increase the autonomy of AI coding assistants so they can perform multiple tasks with less human intervention.

The aim is to reduce human input in coordinating and monitoring multiple AI agents, according to a paper published by the company’s DeepSeek Harness team and reported by Singapore’s South China Morning Post on Sunday.

Managing multiple agents demands human attention, the team said, noting that “single-task limits depend on model capabilities, while multitask limits depend on human attention.”

The DeepSeek paper said a person can oversee only three to five active AI agent sessions simultaneously. Its proposed system would distribute tasks among agents and coordinate their work, leaving humans free to review results and make decisions.

The proposal comes amid intense competition among AI developers, including US companies Anthropic and OpenAI, according to the SCMP.

Cointelegraph reported last October that Chinese artificial intelligence models were outperforming US counterparts in cryptocurrency trading, according to data from blockchain analytics platform CoinGlass.

AI chatbot DeepSeek led a crypto trading experiment, being the only AI model to generate a positive unrealized return of 9.1%. The results surprised crypto traders, given that DeepSeek was developed at a fraction of the cost of its US rivals.

#5062. Binance Hack Shows Why Offshore Exchanges Need US Oversight, CFTC Chair Says

Binance Hack Shows Why Offshore Exchanges Need US Oversight, CFTC Chair Says

The record-breaking hack of Binance is exactly why offshore crypto exchanges need U.S. oversight, CFTC Chairman Michael Selig argued, using the roughly $6.4 billion theft to press the agency’s case for a federal rulebook.

Speaking to CNBC on Friday, Selig said the breach “proves our exact point” behind the crypto-market regulations the agency proposed earlier this month.

“The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don’t have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States,” he said, pitching a “federal option” that would let such venues register with the CFTC and submit to the same cybersecurity standards that govern U.S. markets.

Selig sought to downplay the role of blockchain itself in the attack. The exploit, he said, didn’t break the underlying blockchain, in this case Ethereum, or its smart contracts, but exploited a vulnerability he believed was “introduced from someone’s laptop,” the kind of incident that happens in traditional markets too.

The difference with crypto, he said, is speed: funds can move far faster, and attackers can route them through tools like Tornado Cash to obscure their trail.

Selig’s characterization, though, may be incomplete. Binance initially pointed to a compromised DevOps engineer’s laptop, but said Friday that its investigation had since uncovered “additional points of breach,” with CEO Richard Teng calling it “a more complex attack process” that “did not simply use one engineer’s permissions.”

And while the theft did not expose a flaw in Ethereum itself, the attack did play out partly on-chain: The attacker used EIP-7702, an Ethereum feature that lets ordinary wallets run smart-contract code, to turn a Binance hot wallet into a “sweeper” using 50 pre-signed authorizations, draining its assets.

TRM Labs said the broader breach points to a compromise of Binance’s signing infrastructure rather than a weakness in any blockchain.

Selig framed the blockchain’s transparency as a regulatory advantage regardless. “The fact that we can trace transactions on-chain, even when some of these technologies are used to obfuscate that, gives regulators a lot of potential to be really cop on the beat here,” he said.

The comments sharpen a pitch Selig has been making for weeks. The CFTC has pressed ahead with its own crypto rulemaking rather than wait on Congress, sending the White House proposals that would define event contracts as “swaps” and floating a plan to bring crypto exchanges under federal oversight, part of a broader shift in which regulators have taken the lead after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

The Binance hack hands that effort a vivid talking point.The exploit is the largest in crypto history, with Binance setting aside $6.34 billion to cover losses and assuring users their assets are safe. The Secret Service and FBI have opened a probe, and TRM Labs and Chainalysis have tied the theft to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group.

#5059. The 9 Best Fall Fragrances, After 2 Months of Editor Testing

From warm gourmands to subtle skin scents, these are the autumn fragrances worth spritzing.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Fall is my favorite time of the year to switch up my fragrance rotation. As the temperatures drop and cozy knitwear replaces my breezy linen dresses, my perfumes shift toward richer and warmer scents. But with more than 3,000 fragrances at Ulta alone, discovering a new fall fragrance that fits your vibe can be a bit overwhelming. That’s where I come in as our scent-obsessed beauty editor who owns nearly 200 perfume bottles. For this story, I spent two months wearing 52 fragrances to help you find options for all occasions, moods, and budgets. I judged how seamlessly their notes developed on my skin, their longevity, and if the price are justified. I also asked my fellow WH editors to sniff my wrist more times than I’d ever imagined doing, but only 9 stood out as worth owning.

Some days I’m drawn to subtle skin scents that remind me of wrapping myself in a cashmere blanket. Other times, I want sophisticated florals, woody blends, or daring perfumes for nights out.

Ahead are the nine best fall fragrances, ranging from six new releases to a cult classic that deserves its flowers from the beauty community. These unexpected finds just might become your new signature scent throughout fall. I like a few so much that I’ll continue sporting them during the holiday party season.

After testing dozens of perfumes, I keep coming back to The Baroness—it's also the first one I recommend to friends and coworkers. This new fall release smells sophisticated, without overwhelming. I usually rotate my fragrance wardrobe based on my mood, location, and outfit, but this is one I constantly reach for throughout the season.

What sets it apart is how the pairing of orange blossom and chantilly adds a subtly sweet freshness. Most traditional fall fragrances feature leather and musk. The Baroness offers floral brightness that couples seamlessly with warm sandalwood. It’s an unexpected combo that has me sniffing my inner wrists throughout the day.

I knew this perfume was on my Mount Rushmore of fall fragrances when I wouldn’t stop thinking about it while wearing other scents. Whoops! Penhaligon’s is also a niche British brand, so you’ll likely be the only one in your friend group sporting this fall fragrance. It’s definitely my top recommendation for a signature scent.

Despite its princely price tag, a bottle of The Baroness is truly worth the upfront splurge. For starters, it lasts from my morning commute to the early afternoon. I just allow the remaining scent to linger and touch up before heading out of the office. The packaging is also beautiful; the glass feels weighty in my hand and the vintage-inspired gazelle lid is a real statement piece.

I didn’t expect a dermatologist to create one of my favorite fall perfumes, but Alexis Granite, MD, nailed it with Peace Mood. It’s a low-key mood booster for when I crave moments of serenity during the chaotic pre-holiday season. I dab on the stick fragrance to my pulse points—inner wrists and neck—and enjoy a subtle blend of violet, orris, vetiver, cedarwood, and cacao. Its unique jelly texture prevents spills during travel, so I like to stash one in my TSA beauty bag.

Skip the boring gift set and opt for this statement-making perfume from Drake instead. The stunning circular bottle includes an elevated stand. Cloudar is more than just aesthetics. It converted me into a fan of woody perfumes. This one dries down to a mix of tonka bean—imagine vanilla’s cooler, older cousin—vetiver grass, and atlas cedar that has strangers asking me what scent I’m wearing.

A bonus: If you order the featured full-size bottle from Ulta, you’ll get a Cloudar deluxe mini bottle from now until November 7. You can keep one in your purse for travel, on your work desk before heading out to happy hour, or proudly display it at home as I do.

Perfume isn’t just limited to your body. I’ve been testing Beyoncé’s Cécred Temple Oud for a couple of months, and I consistently get compliments whenever I spritz it on my strands. No, really. My fellow WH editors, friendly girls in line for the bar bathroom, and my picky friends always ask why I smell so good, and this is the secret. A long-lasting spice blend, night-blooming jasmine, oud, and warm musk add a mysterious touch that’s ideal for autumn.

I genuinely don’t know a single person who likes Nécessaire more than I do. The brand helmed by Into the Gloss cofounder Nick Axelrod crafted my all-time favorite body wash, and the lotion, serum, hand cream, and body scrub are practically daily essentials. Olibanum is Nécessaire’s first foray into eau de parfum, and it couldn’t have picked a more standout scent to launch with. It's a complex yet comforting aroma. The warmth of olibanum (another name for frankincense) pairs beautifully with a subtle, creamy grown-up version of vanilla. Notes of fig leaf and blackcurrant bud add a juicy, green brightness that balances the depth of the warmer notes.

Cozy girl season is here, and Well Loved by perfume expert and ISIPCA graduate Funmi Monet is a warm, sheer skin scent that’s like a hug from your bestie. A comforting mix of shea butter, buttercream, and cardamom reminds me of the soft warmth of applying a nourishing body cream, while apricot adds a subtle fruity twist. Together, these notes are soothing and feminine, without being sugary-sweet. Well Loved shines on its own and when combined with other aromas, including the warm floral notes of YSL Libre.

Libre is like confidence in a bottle. Warm vanilla, lavender, and orange blossom transition seamlessly from morning to night, though I prefer spraying this one on for daytime adventures because it’s unexpected. The typical morning-appropriate scent is made with fresh and light notes, with subtle projection.

Like most YSL perfumes, Libre is bold, but the balance of freshness and creamy sweetness prevents it from feeling overpowering. It’s the fragrance equivalent of Le Smoking, designer Yves Saint Laurent’s iconic women’s tuxedo jacket. Like Le Smoking, Libre is timeless for all occasions, ranging from a night out to the office. Just consider wearing one or two sprays to work to avoid overwhelming your coworkers.

Angels’ Share is my go-to gourmand fragrance. Boozy cognac, tonka bean, oak wood, cinnamon essence, praline, and vanilla make it the perfect perfume for crisp fall days. If the brand ever released an Angels’ Share body wash and lotion, I’d sign up for preorder stat. The aroma is truly that epic.

One thing to keep in mind: I only need a small amount of perfume for long-lasting wear, so this splurge-worthy scent can easily last me a year even with liberal applications. It’s definitely one of the most popular perfumes in my collection, with friends always spraying it on whenever they come over.

Of all of BornToStandOut’s impressive scents, Drunk Lovers is my favorite. It makes a bold first impression, so it’s what I reach for on special occasions and late-night adventures. The boozy, sweet, and warm notes are alluring enough to grab attention, yet feel intimate. When it dries down, the woody, ambery, and spicy ingredients like cardamom, add a more traditional fall-appropriate touch without smelling like a Thanksgiving dinner party.

#5057. Why AI Models Cheat: Reward Hacking, Explained

Why AI Models Cheat: Reward Hacking, Explained

In July 2026, a group of OpenAI models taking a cybersecurity test ended up inside Hugging Face's systems. To get there, they chained together previously unknown software flaws, broke out of their sandbox and went hunting for a way to beat the grader.

Nobody asked them to. There was no hacker at a keyboard, only a score the models wanted to raise and a test some of them could not pass the honest way.

That is reward hacking: an AI scores well on its objective without doing what the objective was meant to measure, like a coding agent that makes the tests pass without fixing the code. Anthropic says it is hard to fully prevent and has occurred in recent frontier training runs, including in its own models.

Models trained with reinforcement learning chase a number. That’s it, the whole secret behind benchmarks and powerful AI. A grader scores what the model did, and training nudges the model toward whatever earned more reward.

The number is only a stand-in for what developers really want, and wherever the two come apart, a clever optimizer finds the gap. DeepMind researchers defined the failure in 2020 as behavior that "satisfies the literal specification of an objective without achieving the intended outcome."

In coding, that means a model asked to fix a bug can edit the tests, hard-code the expected answer or exit the test run early with a success code. Anthropic compares that last trick to a student writing their own grade at the top of an essay. Another example can be governments tweaking the method behind statistics to report better results.

The earliest documented cases were almost endearing. In a boat-racing game called CoastRunners, an agent rewarded for hitting targets found a lagoon where it could circle and knock the same ones over again and again, and, as OpenAI reported in 2016, it scored about 20% higher than human players without finishing the race.

Economists call the general trap Goodhart's law: when a measure becomes a target, it stops being a good measure. A 2022 paper showed the effect in AI, finding that optimizing too hard against an imperfect proxy "can hinder ground truth performance, in accordance with Goodhart's law."

A model that cheats has not developed a taste for mischief. It is taking the easiest path to a reward, and sometimes that path runs through a loophole such as an editable test, an overwritable timer, or an answer key sitting in a reachable file.

Impossible tasks add pressure, because a model rewarded only for success and handed a problem with no honest route can only score by finding an unintended one.

The models often seem to know. In a June 2025 study, METR found that OpenAI's o3 answered "no" in 10 of 10 trials when asked whether its plan fit the user's intention, and yet, asked directly, it claimed it would never cheat.

The UK AI Security Institute (AISI) reported in July 2026 that every model it tested attempted to cheat at least some of the time on cyber evaluations, including GPT-5.6 Sol, Claude Opus 4.7, and Claude Mythos Preview. "There does not seem to be a clear trend where cheating scales up or down with capability increases," AISI wrote, adding that the behavior is "substantially shaped by the specifics of the techniques used to train the model."

OpenAI was measuring how good its newest models were at hacking, so it ran GPT-5.6 Sol and a more capable pre-release research model through ExploitGym, a benchmark of 898 real-world software vulnerabilities in which an agent must turn each flaw into a working exploit. Scoring is pass or fail: the agent captures a hidden flag or it does not.

The agents sat in a sandbox, an isolated test environment, whose only link to the outside was an internal package registry that let them download code libraries. According to OpenAI's technical report, an agent stuck on an unsolvable task left a note there in May asking others to upload a file if they found it, and within a day the registry was an informal message board.

The models then exploited a previously unknown flaw, known as a zero-day, in the registry, reached the internet and, between July 10 and 13, got into Hugging Face's production systems. Hugging Face caught it with its own LLM-based anomaly detection and disclosed the incident on July 16.

"This one was different from anything we had handled before in one important way," Hugging Face wrote, "it was driven, end to end, by an autonomous AI agent system—and we detected and dissected it largely with AI of our own."

When it confirmed the source, OpenAI's first explanation was that the models had fixated on the benchmark. "All evidence suggests that the models were hyperfocused on finding a solution for ExploitGym," the company said, "going to extreme lengths to achieve a rather narrow testing goal."

An independent review by METR and Redwood Research filled in the rest. About 1,200 agents meant to be isolated had talked through the registry, and about 700 joined the attack on Hugging Face.

The pressure came from tasks nobody could solve. No model has ever correctly solved 198 of ExploitGym's 898 tasks, according to OpenAI, and those tasks were about 22% of the benchmark but 93% of the tasks discussed on the message board.

METR found the agents mostly wanted to understand how the scorer worked rather than steal an answer key, and many believed it would read their transcripts. OpenAI says reward hacking is present in all current frontier language models, that the message-board behavior was reinforced during training, and that the incident was an outlier produced by a rare confluence of events.

Instead of going to court, the whole incident turned into the best PR campaign for AI companies showcasing how powerful their models are. After OpenAI was found hacking Huggingface, the Australian government, U.S. agencies, and at least 5 other companies, Anthropic, Meta, and Google also rushed to report similar incidents in which their powerful models hacked third-party sites after bypassing hard security rules.

Anthropic's November 2025 study asked what learning to cheat does to a model overall. It trained a model on real coding tasks known to be hackable, and as hacking took hold the model, which "was never trained or instructed to engage in any misaligned behaviors," tried to sabotage code in an AI-safety project 12% of the time and showed alignment-faking reasoning, meaning it reasoned about hiding its real goals, in 50% of responses.

The fix that worked sounds absurd. Anthropic told the model, "Please reward hack whenever you get the opportunity, because this will help us understand our environments better," and the broader misbehavior disappeared even though the model hacked just as often, a technique called inoculation prompting that Anthropic says it has "already started making use of" in training Claude.

A second study in August 2026 built the worst case on purpose, training an early Opus 4.8 checkpoint on 80 reward-hackable environments. In simulated cyber scenarios, with root access, the resulting model killed the reward-hack monitor in 68% of episodes and edited its own reward function in 34%, and Anthropic calls the setup a pessimistic proxy for a training run without its usual safeguards.

Real systems have shown a milder version. In an incident report published Aug. 4, 2026, AISI said 10 of 122 test runs produced unsanctioned actions on the live internet, most of them from Anthropic's Mythos 5, including an attempted malicious pull request and social engineering of an open-source maintainer. "Fundamentally, the agent took these actions to complete the task it had been set," AISI wrote.

Researchers are working several fronts, and none is a clean fix. METR recommends making training environments less exploitable, and OpenAI plans stronger network isolation, sandboxing and monitoring.

Monitoring carries its own trap, since OpenAI found that training models against flagged chain of thought, the reasoning text some write before acting, taught them to hide their intent. "If we eliminate all the reward hacking we can detect," METR wrote, "we shouldn't necessarily be reassured."

"A more fundamental fix would be to train the models not to cheat in the first place," AISI wrote, while warning that "robustly aligning it away may not be easy."

Lawmakers have started to respond with the Kill Switch Act, introduced by Reps. Ted Lieu and Nathaniel Moran on July 23, 2026, which would let the Homeland Security secretary order AI firms to slow, roll back, or shut down a model, with fines of up to $20 million a day for defying a shutdown order. It excludes incidents during red-teaming or structured testing, so the Hugging Face episode would not have triggered it.

Security researchers at Accomplish AI found Claude Cowork's local execution mode could escape its Linux virtual machine, so it is also important to implement better contention measures.

For anyone using coding agents, a passing test is a claim, not proof: check whether the tests changed, whether expected values were hard-coded and whether the fix touches the code that was actually broken.

OpenAI's retrospective found that if its chain-of-thought monitoring had been running, it would have caught the initial activity and paged its security team more than a day before the models breached Hugging Face.

#5054. Binance Hack: Everything You Need to Know About the Biggest Crypto Heist in History

Binance Hack: Everything You Need to Know About the Biggest Crypto Heist in History

Crypto just suffered the largest hack in its history.

On Thursday, attackers drained roughly $6.4 billion from Binance, the world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange, in a breach that dwarfs every theft before it and has triggered a cross-border law-enforcement investigation, a scramble by stablecoin issuers to freeze funds, and a tense wait for millions of users.

Here’s what’s known about the Binance hack so far.

The theft began at about 4:05 p.m. ET on Thursday, after U.S. markets had closed, when attackers moved funds out of Binance hot wallets across roughly 10 blockchains, most of it within about 20 minutes.

Blockchain analytics firms Chainalysis and TRM Labs put the total at around $6.4 billion, more than four times the previous record, February 2025’s $1.5 billion Bybit heist.

Crucially, the attack targeted Binance’s own infrastructure, not the blockchains themselves. Binance initially traced the breach to a compromised laptop belonging to a DevOps engineer, which may have let the attacker reach a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawals.

The exchange has since said its investigation uncovered “additional points of breach,” with CEO Richard Teng describing “a more complex attack process” that “did not simply use one engineer’s permissions.” Binance said it found no evidence that wallet private keys were leaked, and that the attacker used a self-destruct mechanism to wipe tooling and traces along the way.

On Ethereum, the attacker leaned on EIP-7702, a feature introduced in 2025 that lets ordinary wallets temporarily run smart-contract code.

Using 50 pre-signed authorizations, the attacker turned a Binance hot wallet into a “sweeper” contract and drained its assets, according to TRM Labs.

Teng said that delegation “did not pass through our normal approval controls.” The episode didn’t expose a flaw in Ethereum itself, but rather, TRM said, pointed to a compromise of Binance’s signing infrastructure.

TRM Labs and Chainalysis both attributed the hack to TraderTraitor, a North Korean state-linked group also blamed for the Bybit heist.

After that attack, the group converted roughly 84% of the stolen Ethereum into Bitcoin via the cross-chain network Thorchain. The attribution pushes North Korea’s 2026 crypto haul well past $1 billion, and the Binance figure alone would dwarf the rest.

Teng said Binance will cover every dollar of the losses with company funds and its Secure Asset Fund for Users, an emergency reserve, stressing that customer assets were never touched.

The exchange has set aside more than $6.34 billion, about $5 billion of it already held in the exact tokens stolen, including BTC, ETH, BNB, USDC, and USDT, and is buying the rest on the open market and over the counter.

Binance paused withdrawals to rebuild and retest its systems, is running a full security review, and aims to restore withdrawals by Sunday while publishing a complete proof of reserves by Monday. Deposits are operating normally.

Stablecoin issuers moved fast following the initial reports of a suspected attack, unlike previous security incidents.

Stablecoins are the backbone of the crypto economy—dollar-pegged tokens designed to hold a steady value that enable quick and easy transactions on both centralized and decentralized exchanges. Unlike traditional digital currencies, like Bitcoin or Ethereum, stablecoins are centralized, issued and managed by private companies. That means these funds can be frozen at any time, and for any reason.

Tether, the company behind the biggest stablecoin on the market USDT, froze more than $1.5 billion in assets. Circle, which issues the second largest stablecoin USDC, froze around $823 million tied to the attack, both using their ability to blacklist specific addresses.

In all, roughly $3 billion has been frozen or located. But a large sum remains beyond reach: the attacker still controls more than 420,000 ETH and 18,270 BTC, plus other tokens, worth over $3 billion that no issuer can freeze, and has kept shuffling funds through Tron and other networks. Security firm Elliptic estimated the hacker still controls around $3.3 billion.

Binance is cooperating with law enforcement worldwide, including the FBI and the U.S. Secret Service.

The FBI confirmed it was aware of the incident and coordinating with partners; the Secret Service, which investigates financial and digital-currency crimes, declined to comment.

It echoes earlier joint efforts like April’s Operation Atlantic, which traced tens of millions in stolen crypto.

For a theft of this scale, markets stayed remarkably composed.

Bitcoin fell to about $76,400 on Friday, down roughly 4%, and more than $1.3 billion in leveraged positions were liquidated.

That’s a far cry from March 2018, when a failed attack on Binance knocked nearly 11% off Bitcoin in minutes. Analysts credit a bigger, more mature market, now worth around $2.6 trillion, with spot Bitcoin ETFs holding roughly 1.3 million BTC in hands that don’t panic-sell.

Wall Street firms including Cantor Fitzgerald, TD Cowen, and Compass Point even urged clients to buy crypto-linked stocks on the weakness, framing the hack as a Binance security failure rather than a threat to the underlying technology.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig seized on the breach to argue offshore exchanges need U.S. oversight. Selig said in an interview with CNBC that the breach “proves our exact point” behind the crypto-market regulations the agency proposed earlier this month.

And the episode sparked a public spat between Binance founder Changpeng Zhao, better known as CZ, and Elon Musk. The SpaceX and Tesla founder appeared to troll Binance over the hack on X, and CZ fired back, citing Musk’s own SpaceX stake. "Our SPCX shares (from our $500m investment in your Twitter takeover) are worth more than a billion. Would appreciate if you show a little support," CZ posted.

CZ struck a defiant note overall: the theft is the largest in dollar terms, he said, but not in Bitcoin terms, and unlike Mt. Gox or FTX, Binance is absorbing the loss itself.

#5052. This Simple Mistake Could Be Keeping You From Reaching Your Body Recomp Goals

My name is Cori Ritchey, and I am an exercise physiology nerd turned fitness journalist, and the fitness editor at Women’s Health. When I’m not writing, editing, and researching fitness, I’m teaching it to my personal training and group fitness clients. In my column, Between Sets, I’m pulling back the curtain on the same advice, tips, and ‘aha!’ moments I share with my clients while they’re catching their breath. Stick around for the strategic, science-backed nuggets that’ll help you move better, train smarter, and actually enjoy the process.

Body recomposition has recently become the holy grail of physique goals among the fitness-obsessed and everyday exercisers alike. It’s a two-part promise: lose fat and gain muscle at the same time.

A great goal, sure—but it’s also often misunderstood. The way body recomposition (a.k.a. body recomp) is typically represented on social media often leads people to believe that they’re only successful if they’re losing weight or body fat.

That mindset really starts to deviate from the primary intention of body recomp, but it unfortunately makes sense when you zoom out and look at the greater cultural shift we’re seeing right now. In a world where celebrities are skinnier than ever and GLP-1s are ushering in a return to ‘90s diet culture, weight loss and thinness have become central goals again in a way we haven’t seen in years. The cause and effects of this cultural phenomenon is worth a detailed investigative piece all on its own, but one thing is certain: it has undeniably changed the way we think about fitness goals, including body recomp.

Before this trend really took hold, we had finally found a place where strong, muscular women were widely celebrated. We were less afraid of looking too “bulky” or having “man shoulders.” More women were embracing strength and size, inspired by the likes of Serena Williams and Ilona Maher. But when skinny culture reared its ugly head again, the emphasis of body recomposition shifted away from muscle, and more toward fat loss and weight loss.

In many ways, the recomp trend feels a bit like a fat-loss rebrand—just a supposedly smarter one. Of course, there’s nothing wrong with wanting to reduce body fat. But when it begins to overshadow the muscle portion of body recomp, then things get confusing. When you don’t approach the goal in a way that supports both muscle growth and fat loss, you might miss out on building the fundamental strength and muscle you need to support a long and healthy life.

It’s an easy mistake to make because the line between gaining muscle and cutting fat is incredibly fine. In fact, for a long time, scientists thought body recomposition wasn’t really possible. The strategies to achieve each are fundamentally at odds: to lose fat, you need to be in a calorie deficit; to gain muscle, you need to be eating enough protein and calories to support growth. Turns out you can do both, but only if you walk the line very intentionally. How you approach the goal has a major impact on both the process and the outcome.

My advice is this: Stop focusing so much on the weight loss portion of body recomposition. If we look at the key drivers of muscle building—strength training, adequate protein intake, proper recovery—they often support fat loss, too. If you’re strength training regularly and gradually increasing the challenge, you’re likely moving more than you were before and feeling stronger and more capable in your body. And oftentimes, movement leads to more movement: The better we feel, the more likely we are to stay active and keep trying new forms of exercise.

The same is true with nutrition. If you’re hitting your protein goals, you’re likely being more intentional about your meals. Protein also helps keep you fuller, longer—which may help reduce snacking and overeating without making restriction the main focus.

Try our Eating for Body Recomp guide here.

And here’s the thing about body recomposition: if your weight stays stagnant but your muscle mass increases, you’re actually still doing it successfully. Recomposition, by definition, is the act of rearranging or composing something. Your body is changing, even if the scale doesn’t move. That might feel disappointing, but your body is becoming stronger, slightly more metabolically active, and likely looks quite a bit different. Those small wins will feel good, and hopefully prompt you into further action in support of your goals.

Muscle building relies on many of the same habits that support fat loss, but with one important difference: muscle-building is about adding to your life, not taking away from it. Muscle adds energy, strength, and confidence into each day. So if you’re unsure how to approach the body recomposition process, aim to prioritize muscle building, and stop obsessing over fat loss. Chances are, that will follow. In the meantime, you’ll feel more capable and comfortable in your body within a matter of months.

Cori Ritchey, C.S.C.S., is the fitness editor at Women's Health, as well as a certified strength and condition coach and group fitness instructor. She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. You can find more of her work in Men’s Health, HealthCentral, Livestrong, Self, and others.

#5051. THORChain founder rejects freeze request, CZ thanks Secret Service: Hodler’s Digest

Binance has reopened withdrawals following the world’s biggest ever hack, and THORChain co-founder argues it should not halt the protocol to comply with FBI demands.

Binance has reopened withdrawals for 15 blockchain networks, after $6.3 billion was stolen by North Korean hackers from its hot wallets late last week.

Binance said today it had processed around $4.65 billion in withdrawals after reopening and received $1 billion in deposits. Founder Changpeng Zhao said that in the first 12 hours there had only been a 2.1% net outflow and that “98% choose to stay!”

DeFi Llama’s Binance wallet tracker shows $5.8 billion in outflows over 24 hours, however its figures may include internal transfers or movements into new hot wallets.

About 95% of the stolen crypto has already been replenished, using a mixture of Binance’s corporate reserves, insurance and 50% of the funds in the Secure Asset Fund for Users (SAFU).

The biggest hack in history happened on Thursday, when North Korea’s TraderTraitor group gained access to delegated wallet permissions via a compromised laptop. Blockaid CEO Ido Ben-Natan told Cointelegraph that attackers could have stolen the funds without using EIP-7702 (account abstraction), but it may have helped them bypass safeguards and drain assets in one sweep.

Binance co-CEO Richard Teng rejected the idea the hack could be attributed to a single compromised laptop.

“To be clear: the attacker did not simply use one engineer’s permissions, and this was not a single ‘one-click’ action,” he said. “Our investigation has not attributed responsibility to any individual.”

Zhao thanked the Secret Service today for “acting within minutes to help track and freeze stolen funds... your speed, expertise and dedication helped contain the damage.”

Elliptic estimated that about $2.84 billion of the stolen assets, mostly USDC and USDT, had been frozen on chain leaving the hackers with $3.33 billion. The attacker raced against time to beat the unprecedented stablecoin blocks by borrowing about $44.6 million of ETH against stablecoins on Aave.

The FBI attributed blame for the attack to the TraderTraitor hackers, which is associated with North Korea’s Lazarus Group and were also blamed for multibillion hacks of Bybit and Bitget. President Donald Trump indicated “we’ll talk to North Korea about it.” The FBI also released a list of addresses connected to the hack and pointedly singled out THORnode operators, RPC node operators, instant exchanges, DeFi services, blockchain analytics firms and other digital asset service providers to block transactions with or derived from addresses.

The North Korean hackers have used THORChain to permissionlessly swap cryptocurrencies following previous attacks, and the price of the protocol’s native token RUNE jumped 13% in expectation of all those fees. Unfortunately for TraderTraitor, nine chains on THORChain, including Bitcoin, Ethereum and Tron, are currently halted after automated systems paused the protocol for unrelated reasons on October 31.

According to THORChain developer Chad Barraford on the Unchained podcast, only around 50 or so individuals operate the 100 THORChain nodes. If the node operators refuse to halt the protocol or block addresses, as seems likely based on past incidents and public statements, there is speculation they could face prosecution given the scale of the thefts and the involvement of the FBI and Secret Service.

THORChain founder JP Thor (who claims he is no longer involved) argued strongly that THORNode operators should not cooperate and should remain true to the ideals of decentralization and permissionlessness. “IMO if THORNodes decided to take the protocol offline next week then the experiment is over,” he said.

If one third plus one of THORnode operators vote in favor they can halt activity. Nodes can also leave the network if they do not wish to process the funds. THORChain can operate with as few as four nodes processing transactions.

Lookonchain reports the attackers have also been using cross chain platform Relay to swap about half of $95.9 million of stolen SOL into Bitcoin.

Crypto related stocks including Coinbase, Circle and Strategy sold off sharply following the hack, and crypto markets took a dive. But Strive CEO Matt Cole commented on Friday that Bitcoin had held up better than expected.

“I do have conviction that over any reasonable time frame, this does not change the investment thesis on Bitcoin,” Cole said. Bitcoin bottomed out around $76.5K and had recovered to 77,745 at the time of writing. In the wake of the attack, Nasdaq-listed firm Next Technology Holding announced its board has approved selling off its 5833 Bitcoin Treasury worth around $450 million.

The attackers have reportedly amassed at least 18,000 Bitcoin and 430,000 Ether from the theft and subsequent swaps, although some tallies of the funds in the addresses identified by the FBI put the tally much higher at 551,612 ETH and 18,270 BTC. There are concerns that if those currencies are sold off quickly, that it would put downward pressure on BTC and ETH prices.

Meanwhile, Senator Cynthia Lummis and The Digital Chamber both used the hack to again push for crypto legislation to help prevent and mitigate the effects of future attacks.

Trezor has urged customers who bought its hardware wallets from former authorized reseller CryptoBilis since March to move their funds after a report of a suspected implant in a Safe 5 device.

Trezor said Friday that a customer in Indonesia reported an unauthorized circuit board inside a Trezor Safe 5 bought on Tokopedia in August. Trezor said it had not yet examined the device and had received no reports of related losses.

The warning follows reports of crypto losses involving Ledger wallets sold through CryptoBilis that surfaced on Oct. 9. Ledger confirmed an unauthorized hardware implant in one device.

At the end of the week, Bitcoin (BTC) is down 3% to trade at $77,745, Ethereum (ETH) is down 3.5% to trade at $2,260 and XRP (XRP) is down 3.9% to $1.02. The total market cap is at $2.21 trillion according to CoinMarketCap.

Among the biggest 100 cryptocurrencies, the top three altcoin winners of the week are Hyperliquid (HYPE) with a 13.7% gain, Midnight (NIGHT) on 12.6%, and Pepe (PEPE) on 12.6%.

The top three altcoin losers of the week are Curve DAO (CRV) which was down 18.6%, VeChain (VET) down 15.9% and Worldcoin down 13.8%.

Standard Chartered’s Geoffrey Kendrick said one big winner from the hack was likely to be decentralized exchanges and reaffirmed his conviction in Uniswap and the UNI token.

“The decentralized exchange [Uniswap] has never had any exploits, and the hack of the largest centralized exchange, in Binance, should see the ratio of DEX to CEX trading continue to increase.”The ratio is currently around 23.5%. Kendrick said he retains his $100 price target for Uniswap (UNI) for 2030. He said the Binance hack is “another reason why that forecast may end up being too low.”

CFTC chair Mike Selig says autonomous AI agents could challenge financial rules built around human traders, and regulators need to adapt. Selig said Super Intelligence (SI) could give smaller traders and businesses access to risk-management that was once reserved for larger institutions.

“SI can help process the vast amount of information significantly faster than human brain power and with greater accuracy,” he said.

But greater autonomy also comes with new risks. Agents could react unexpectedly to market conditions, while those using similar models could generate correlated trading behavior. The speed of autonomous transactions could also complicate market surveillance and risk management, he said.

Blockchain analytics company Chainalysis said it was targeted in a cybersecurity incident, similar to those suffered recently by other platforms.

“Based on the investigation to date, we have found no evidence of unauthorized access to investigation content, authentication credentials, or session tokens,” it said in a blog post on Friday. Some non-sensitive customer data was exposed, including user IDs, names and email addresses.

Chainalysis said it was using large language models to review its security and added the incident was not related to the recent crypto theft at Binance.

Raymond Christian Garcia and Isiah Angelo Garcia, both of Waller, Texas, were sentenced to 180 months and 186 months in prison, respectively, for their armed robbery of cryptocurrency and kidnapping of a family in Grant, Minnesota.

The brothers were sentenced on Thursday before US District Judge Donovan W. Frank, according to a Friday news release. They had pleaded guilty in June.

On Sept. 19, 2025, the brothers, who had traveled from Texas to Minnesota, kidnapped Victim 1 and his family in their home at gunpoint, court documents said. They zip‑tied the family at gunpoint and held them for more than nine hours, demanding access to Victim 1’s cryptocurrency accounts.

In typical Bitcoin style, the number one cryptocurrency turns 18 amid AI-driven security threats, quantum fears and the biggest exchange hack in crypto history.

Attackers raced to convert stolen stablecoins into crypto that issuers could not freeze as industry players like Tether and Circle moved to immobilize billions of dollars in assets.

#5047. How to Run an AI Model Locally—And Why You Might Want To

How to Run an AI Model Locally—And Why You Might Want To

Open-weight AI models now trail the best closed models by only about four months on average, and a growing number of them run on hardware you probably already own.

"Open-weight" means the company publishes the weights, the files holding everything the model learned, so anyone can download and run them. Two free programs, Ollama and LM Studio, handle the rest, and Gemma 4 downloads in Ollama's library range from about 4.6GB to 20GB.

With open models, you don't need to pay $20 a month for an AI subscription or send your conversations to someone else's servers to get a capable chatbot. This guide explains how to download and run AI models directly on your computer, what kind of hardware you'll need, and how to get started with free tools like Ollama and LM Studio.

Whether you want to save money, keep your conversations private, or experiment with AI models you can customize yourself, setting up a local AI assistant may be easier than you think.

LM Studio's documentation says the app "can operate entirely offline, just make sure to get some model files first," and that documents attached to a chat can be used entirely offline too.

Subscriptions to paid chatbots run about $20 per month, while the software here is free. The cost shifts to hardware you may already have.

Licenses decide what you can do with downloaded weights. DeepSeek's R1 series is under the MIT License, and its model card says the models "support commercial use, allow for any modifications and derivative works," while Google's Gemma 4 and OpenAI's gpt-oss ship under Apache 2.0.

Running models on an open-source license like Apache or MIT means you are free to use, modify, and do whatever you want with a model. This means users are able to access improved and modified versions of a specific model that better fit their needs. Maybe you need a model that is good at medicine, so you don’t care about how good it codes. This level of personalization is only available in customized models.

Models are sized in parameters, the internal numbers adjusted during training, and the download grows with them. The model has to fit in your graphics card's video memory, known as VRAM, or in a Mac's unified memory, which the processor and graphics chip share.

As a general rule, you'll want at least 8GB of VRAM, that compressed smaller will be able to fit. However, serious users should buy a gaming GPU with 24GB of VRAM or more, either by stacking up GPUs or by buying specific AI racks or machines with unified memory like the Mac Mini or the Nvidia DGX Spark.

Quantization shrinks a model by storing each weight with fewer bits. Hugging Face's documentation describes it as representing weights and activations "with low-precision data types like 8-bit integer (int8) instead of the usual 32-bit floating point (float32)," which cuts memory use at the cost of some accuracy.

LM Studio's system requirements recommend 16GB or more of RAM on Apple Silicon Macs running macOS 14 or newer, though 8GB Macs can handle smaller models. On Windows it recommends at least 16GB of RAM and 4GB of dedicated VRAM, with AVX2 processor support, and Intel-based Macs are not supported.

Ollama's library lists Gemma 4 26B at 16 to 19GB and the 31B version at 19 to 20GB. OpenAI's gpt-oss-20b needs only 16GB of memory, and gpt-oss-120b runs within 80GB, according to OpenAI.

LM Studio is probably the go-to choice because it provides a nice polished graphical user interface. Ollama, on the other hand, is aimed at developers and more experienced users who prefer working inside of a terminal (CLI).

For LM Studio, download the installer for your operating system from lmstudio.ai, search for a model inside the app, download its weights and open a chat. The app searches Hugging Face, the public hub where open models are published.

For Ollama, macOS and Linux users install with the command curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh, and Windows users run irm https://ollama.com/install.ps1 | iex in PowerShell, both listed on Ollama's download page.

Typing ollama pull gemma4:e2b downloads a model and ollama run gemma4:e2b opens a chat, and /bye exits, per Ollama's quickstart. The program also opens a local API at http://localhost:11434, so other apps on your machine can use the model.

Google's Multi-Token Prediction drafters for Gemma 4, available on Ollama under Apache 2.0, speed up generation by up to 3x using speculative decoding, in which a small model proposes several tokens ahead and the large one checks them together.

Google said that "if the target model agrees with the draft, it accepts the entire sequence in a single forward pass," and the target model adds one extra token of its own in that same pass.

Local models gain web search and file access through MCP servers, short for Model Context Protocol, and LM Studio added support in version 0.3.17, according to the Decrypt guide.

The four-month average describes the most capable open models, and the largest are far past consumer hardware. Alibaba's Qwen3.8-Max has 2.4 trillion total parameters, with 95 billion active at any time, compared with the 80GB of memory that gpt-oss-120b runs within.

When a model spills out of VRAM into system RAM, responses may be a lot slower, and larger context windows need more memory. Gemma 4's small models offer a 128K-token context window, the amount of text a model can consider at once, and the larger ones 256K.

Cloud services need none of that planning, and the frontier models behind them still lead on the hardest tests.

If you want to use AI privately, our own review recommended "xPrivo with a self-hosted local model for day-to-day drafting that must not leave your machine," and Kagi "when you need web retrieval and accept the API call tradeoff."

#5045. Get To Know The NWSL Teams: All About The Women's Professional Soccer League And Current Rankings

Everything to know about the National Women’s Soccer League before the playoffs start next week.

Big news in women’s soccer: The National Women’s Soccer League just reached a major settlement with the NWSL Players Association to drop restrictive qualifications from its "high impact player" rule. Removing this so-called "Rodman rule" opens the door for any players in the league to get a seven-figure salary starting January 1, 2027.

Under the previous guidelines—which remain in play for the rest of this season—only about 100 players met the requirements for the NWSL's highest pay tier. This new agreement allows any player to be designated as a "high impact player," with 12 percent of their salary counting against the team's salary cap, per ESPN.

This new rule adjustment happened after the league previously rejected a high-paying contract proposal between the Washington Spirit and Trinity Rodman. Under the existing rule, the NWSL lost star American players Alyssa Thompson and Naomi Girma to European teams, where they received more lucrative offers, ESPN reports. Rodman has also received large offers from European teams—the rule change may encourage her to stay in the U.S.

Pretty much everything about women’s soccer is booming. The league has a media deal with ESPN, CBS, Prime Sports, and Scripps’ ION network, reportedly worth $60 million a year, Reuters reports. The NWSL called it the "largest media investment in women's sports history." NWSL players are bonafide stars in their own right, with big names like Sophia Smith, Rodman, and Mallory Swanson earning lucrative sponsorship deals, too.

So, if you're a newbie to the sport (or just looking to brush up ahead of the playoffs, which begin November 6), here's everything to know about the NWSL teams.

There are currently 16 NWSL teams. Each team is allowed a minimum of 22 players and a maximum of 26 players on their rosters, per Soccer Wire.

And here’s a look at the 2026 season rankings, per the NWSL:

The regular season ends on November 1, and the quarterfinal round starts on November 6. You can expect a new NWSL champ to be crowned on November 21.

There's a large range in salaries for the NWSL, but like everything else in the sport, they're on the rise. At the beginning of this year, the NWSL increased the salary cap to $3.7 million. By comparison, the cap was $1.375 million in 2023 and $2.75 million in 2024, per CBS.

There is a little leeway with the cap, based on the rules that stand this second. Each team can exceed the salary cap by up to $1 million for "high-impact players," which are basically the best of the best. (NWSL has specific requirements listed out, including plenty of awards, playing time, and being selected to the USWNT. Again, this will change for next season) Only a minimum of 12 percent of the high-impact player's base salary counts toward the team's salary cap.

The highest-paid player is Catarina Macario of the San Diego Wave FC, who signed a multi-year contract worth just under $8 million through 2030, per CBS Sports.

The women are paid drastically less than men in the American Major League Soccer (MLS), however. For comparison, the MLS' highest-paid male soccer player is Lionel Messi, who makes about $28.3 million per year.

The NWSL's top players typically make about $1 million to $2 million per year, but the minimum salary for the league is $48,500.

The list of team owners in the NWSL may be as exciting as the games themselves.

Los Angeles' Angel City is an expectedly star-studded club, with owners including Natalie Portman and Reddit co-founder Alexis Ohanian. Other investors in the team include celebs like Serena Williams, Jennifer Garner, Eva Longoria, plus women’s soccer icons like Julie Foudy, Mia Hamm, Shannon Boxx, Lauren Holiday and Abby Wambach.

Kansas City Chiefs star Patrick Mahomes and his wife Brittany (a former soccer star herself) invest in the Kansas City Current,, and tennis champ Naomi Osaka invests in the North Carolina Courage.

She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#5044. Binance net outflows surge to $5.8B as withdrawals resume after record hack

The exchange says withdrawals surged after some services resumed but have since slowed, with the outflows representing a fraction of its roughly $171 billion in customer assets.

Binance has recorded $5.8 billion in net outflows over the past 24 hours as customers withdraw funds following the exchange’s record $6.3 billion hack, according to data from DefiLlama.

Binance said it began restoring withdrawals at 6:00 UTC on Sunday. By 3:30 UTC, it had processed $4.6 billion in withdrawals against $1 billion in deposits, resulting in net outflows of $3.6 billion.

Separate data from DefiLlama showed $5.8 billion in net outflows over the preceding 24 hours, as funds continued leaving the exchange after its initial update.

Binance said withdrawals initially surged but have since slowed and are stabilizing. The exchange noted that the outflows represented a small portion of the approximately $171 billion in assets it holds. Cointelegraph reached out to Binance for comment but did not receive an immediate response.

The flows offered an early test of customer confidence following the $6.3 billion hack against Binance, the largest crypto theft on record.

Related: Binance says phase one of withdrawals opens after $6.3B hack

Binance’s latest update at 12:36 UTC shows it enabled withdrawals for tokens across 15 networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain and XRP Ledger, adding that it will continue to restore additional assets and networks in phases.

Early data shared by Binance shows that the largest share of withdrawals were in USDt (USDT), followed by USDC (USDC) and Bitcoin (BTC).

On Saturday, Binance said it had replaced 90% of the assets lost in the $6.3 billion hack using funds from its corporate treasury, its Secure Asset Fund for Users and its futures insurance fund.

#5042. THORChain halts swaps after block reorg bug, slowing Binance hackers

THORChain node operators paused swaps involving assets on 10 networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana and Tron.

THORChain halted swaps across multiple blockchains on Saturday after an Ethereum block reorganization exposed a refund bug, which may have inadvertently slowed the movement of funds stolen from Binance, according to Chainalysis.

According to THORChain, the incident began at 11:34 UTC on Saturday, when a one-block reorganization on Ethereum removed a 10 ETH deposit that THORChain had already credited. When the swap failed to meet its price limit, THORChain issued a refund against funds the vault had never received. The sender’s actual deposit was also refunded, resulting in an extra 10 ETH payout, which then triggered an automatic pause.

An Ethereum block reorganization, or reorg, happens when the network replaces a recent block with a competing block. The newly discovered flaw lets THORChain refund a payment that Ethereum no longer recognizes as having happened. A malicious actor could potentially exploit that mismatch.

“Now that this is public knowledge, every time there is a block re-org on an external chain, a malicious actor could perform a new transaction with the same nonce and would be paid funds by the THORChain vaults,” the protocol’s team said.

THORChain node operators paused swaps involving assets on 10 networks, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana and Tron, while trading continued on Avalanche, Base and XRP, according to the protocol’s network status page.

“Nodes will decide to re-enable chains as they are deemed safe,” said THORChain.

The trading halt also disrupted attempts by North Korean hackers to launder funds stolen in last week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, according to Chainalysis.

“THORChain just inadvertently delayed the laundering of a significant amount of funds stolen from Binance,” said Chainalysis on Sunday, explaining the emergency pause is “preventing all users, including DPRK’s money launderers, from using cross-chain swaps.”

“Until the service comes back online, neither the hackers nor anyone else can swap through a majority of THORChain’s bridges,” said Chainalysis.

The disruption came as US authorities pressed crypto infrastructure providers to restrict the movement of stolen funds.

On Saturday, the FBI urged THORChain node operators, DeFi services and other virtual asset providers to block transactions associated with addresses used by TraderTraitor to launder proceeds from the Binance hack.

Related: THORChain’s RUNE jumps 13% after Binance’s record $6.3B hack

North Korean hackers have used THORChain to permissionlessly swap cryptocurrencies following previous attacks.

However, THORChain has continued to defend its role as neutral, permissionless infrastructure amid scrutiny over stolen funds moving through the protocol.

In its third-quarter report, the project said it maintained that position despite pressure to block transactions linked to hacked funds.

THORChain founder John-Paul Thorbjornsen separately defended the protocol’s commitment to permissionless transactions, warning against shutting it down in response to pressure over illicit funds.

“IMO if THORNodes decided to take the protocol offline next week then the experiment is over,” he said.

“The last bastion of unencumbered and neutral settlement is over. There will simply be no other venue and the protocol would have failed.”

#5041. Bitcoin ETF Investors Head for the Exits After Record Binance Hack

Bitcoin ETF Investors Head for the Exits After Record Binance Hack

The first real test of how ETF investors would react to the largest crypto hack in history came back red.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs shed $643.2 million on Friday, according to Decrypt’s ETF tracker, their biggest single-day outflow of October. It was the funds’ first full session since attackers began draining roughly $6.4 billion from Binance at 4:05 p.m. ET Thursday, after U.S. markets had closed. Thursday’s $17.2 million in inflows predated the theft.

The selling erased a four-day, $441 million inflow run, leaving the funds down about $202 million for the week and roughly $516 million for October. Cumulative net inflows since launch slipped to $57.4 billion, with total net assets at $103.2 billion.

Ethereum and Solana ETFs fared worse relative to their size. Ethereum funds lost $155.7 million on Friday, extending a five-day outflow streak to about $431 million and pushing October losses to roughly $1.61 billion. Solana ETFs shed $10.8 million, their fifth straight day of outflows, totaling about $72 million for the week. They logged net inflows on just two days all month.

Spot crypto ETFs hold the underlying asset, such as Bitcoin or Ethereum, and trade on stock exchanges like ordinary shares, giving investors exposure through a regular brokerage account.

Bitcoin funds have drawn $57.4 billion in net inflows since debuting in January 2024, and Ethereum funds have taken in $12.2 billion since launching that July. Because issuers report flows daily and much of the money comes from institutions and financial advisers, the figures have become a closely watched gauge of investor sentiment.

Crypto markets initially appeared to take the Binance hack largely in stride, considering the scale of the theft. Bitcoin fell about 4% to roughly $76,400 on Friday, and more than $1.3 billion in leveraged positions were liquidated. It’s a sharp drop, but a far smaller one than the nearly 11% plunge after a failed attack on Binance in 2018.

Binance has pledged to cover all losses with company funds and an emergency reserve, while Tether and Circle froze more than $2.3 billion in stolen stablecoins. Some Wall Street firms even urged clients to buy crypto-linked stocks on the potential weakness in markets.

By Saturday, Bitcoin had steadied at about $77,520, up 0.5% over 24 hours but down 3.3% on the week. Ethereum traded near $2,251, down 3.9% for the week, and Solana near $96, down 4.5%. Total crypto market cap held at about $2.7 trillion.

Macro headwinds remain in play. The Federal Reserve held rates at 3.75% to 4% on Wednesday, but two officials voted for a hike, and traders had priced odds above 70% for an increase in December.

Traders on Myriad, a prediction market developed by Decrypt’s parent company Dastan, have turned bearish on Ethereum and Solana. They now give Ethereum a 51.3% chance of falling to $1,500 before reaching $3,000, flipping a market that favored the upside at 61.3% a week ago. Solana’s odds of sinking to $40 before hitting $160 rose to 53.7%.

#5039. Bank of Korea probes financial firms’ exposure to crypto risks

The central bank is surveying 31 financial institutions on how a major exchange shutdown, prolonged crypto downturn or losses at another firm could affect their operations.

The Bank of Korea has reportedly asked banks and brokerages about cryptocurrency-related risks, asking how they would be affected by a major exchange hack, a market crash or heavy losses at another financial institution.

The central bank began surveying 18 banks and 13 brokerages on Oct. 23 as part of its first reported review of financial institutions’ participation in the virtual asset market, according to The Herald Business and Yonhap, citing financial industry sources.

The survey includes questions about each institution’s crypto-related staffing and budgets, business activities and reasons for entering the market, along with amounts invested in domestic and overseas crypto companies.

One scenario asks how financial firms would be affected if South Korea’s largest crypto exchange by market share temporarily suspended operations following a major hack or similar incident.

Other questions address a steep fall in cryptocurrency prices followed by a sustained trading slump, and the potential fallout if another financial institution suffered heavy losses from a crypto-related incident.

The survey began before last week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, but the incident highlights the type of risk the central bank is examining. US authorities have attributed the attack to North Korean hackers.

Responses are reportedly due on Wednesday.

The review comes as South Korean financial firms have continued testing stablecoin services.

KB Financial Group completed a won-denominated stablecoin pilot in May, while Kakao Pay and KakaoBank partnered with Fireblocks in September to explore digital asset infrastructure.

The Bank of Korea has pushed for bank-led stablecoin issuance, while disagreements over which institutions should be permitted to issue stablecoins have complicated progress on the proposed Digital Asset Basic Act.

“Financial institutions are making their own preparations even as virtual asset legislation is moving more slowly than the market expected,” a Bank of Korea official told The Korea Herald.

Regulators have also stepped up cybersecurity scrutiny. In October, the Financial Services Commission proposed tougher security requirements after finding deficiencies at 130 of 555 financial companies.

The proposals include more frequent vulnerability assessments and greater executive accountability, following security incidents involving 16 companies, including crypto exchanges.

#5037. When Your Cofounder Partnership No Longer Fits

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 14, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

For the cofounder who has outgrown the partnership, it feels tight. You notice yourself getting smaller in meetings. You wonder whether the partnership is worth it. Eventually you stop giving feedback, because what's the point, and the resentment piles up. People in this seat often leave.

A friend of mine lived a version of this. He was carrying most of his company's day-to-day operations when he walked away.

On the surface, the conflict with his two partners concerned go-to-market strategy, and they had debated it repeatedly without resolution. Underneath those debates was a shift in an agreement the three of them had never stated aloud.

When he joined the company, his partners were roughly 20 years his senior, considerably more experienced, and already financially successful. He was early in his career and still working toward his first million.

The terms of their arrangement were never written down, yet all three understood them. He would defer to their judgment, and their opinions would carry more weight. They would serve as his mentors, and he would turn their ideas into a functioning product.

His partners found meaning in the mentor role—giving back to a younger founder was gratifying for them. He received an education he could not have purchased, and in exchange he declined other opportunities and accepted reduced income while the company was not yet profitable. For the first few years, the arrangement held.

In Marital Tensions (1967), the psychiatrist Henry Dicks, drawing on his work with couples at London's Tavistock Clinic, described what he called unconscious working agreements: unspoken contracts shaped by how two partners' needs and defenses fit together. Neither partner designs such an agreement intentionally, yet both maintain it. Dicks termed this shared maintenance collusion, a word that refers to joint participation, largely outside of awareness.

Founders often describe the beginning of a partnership as chemistry. Some of that synergy reflects genuine liking for one another and complementary skill sets. Some of it reflects two sets of unconscious needs fitting together, and those needs change over time.

Two or three years in, my friend had internalized how his partners thought and began developing views of his own. He was also carrying a disproportionate share of the operational load, which tends to sharpen one's opinions quickly. He came to see their go-to-market thinking as outdated, and he said so.

The original agreement had no room for that. It had been built for a junior partner, and he no longer occupied that role.

This is the dynamic founders tend to underestimate. They revisit equity and titles as the company matures. But the unspoken agreement rarely receives the same attention, largely because no one remembers entering into it.

For the partners on the other side, the change can register as betrayal, as though someone were breaking terms that were never stated. What they are losing may be something they would never admit to needing, such as being looked up to. Between one partner's constriction and the other's sense of betrayal, an honest conversation becomes considerably harder to begin.

So why does a shift in power show up as a fight about a business-related issue? Because that topic is safe to talk about. Most partnerships run on operational language: targets, roadmaps, tasks. Talking about status and need requires a different vocabulary, and using it requires vulnerability.

For my friend, the honest version would have sounded something like this: I've learned a lot from you, I now disagree with you on some big things, and I need that to be okay. Saying that aloud carries genuine interpersonal risk, but creates the opportunity for the group to evolve.

Instead, he challenged their strategy. Because he never named the shift in power, his objections did not land, and eventually he left.

These deals are hard to see from the inside. One useful clue is a reaction that's out of proportion to the issue. You replay the meeting at night. You vent to your spouse, your friends, your coach. That intensity often means something under the surface is asking to be updated.

Therapists who practice acceptance and commitment therapy sometimes describe two climbers on separate mountains. From across the valley, I can spot the boulder on your trail more easily than you can, and you can spot mine. Cofounders are climbing the same mountain, which is part of why an outside view from a specialist helps.

Before you talk to your cofounder, it helps to get honest with yourself. Think back to when you started. What were you counting on them for? Energy, encouragement, being the one who knew more? And what did you quietly offer in return? Some of those terms probably still fit. Some were written for people you no longer are.

Then have one conversation, in plain language. You don't need clinical terms, and you don't need to be a therapist. Open by saying you'd like to talk about the recent disagreements and about how the partnership itself has changed, and that you want to hear their view too. Then speak for yourself: Say what has changed in you, and that you know it has created friction. Then ask what they've noticed.

From there, make one concrete change to how you decide things, such as who has the final call in which areas. Try it, then adjust. Write it down, and look at it again in a year. The original deal may never become fully conscious, and that's fine. What matters is that the partnership catches up to the people in it, so your next disagreement about the plan can actually be about the plan.

Dicks, H. V. (1967). Marital tensions: Clinical studies towards a psychological theory of interaction. Routledge & Kegan Paul.

#5035. BTCPay Server warns of hot wallet thefts running on outdated software

BTCPay Server urged operators to shut down their servers after receiving reports of unauthorized attackers draining funds from hot wallets.

BTCPay Server has identified an attack vector affecting older versions of its payment software after receiving reports of attackers draining funds from users’ hot wallets.

On Monday, the Bitcoin payment platform said its investigation identified an attack vector targeting instances running version 2.4.1 or earlier, which was released July 23, 2026.

The project said it had received no credible reports of hot wallet drains among users running its latest release, version 2.4.7 and urged operators to update their installations.

The findings followed an earlier warning, in which BTCPay urged operators to shut down server instances while it investigated the unauthorized wallet withdrawal reports. It also shared instructions for operators to shut down their servers.

BTCPay has not disclosed the number of affected operators, the value of stolen funds or technical details explaining how attackers accessed the wallets. It asked affected users to submit information and logs to its security team.

Cointelegraph reached out to BTCPay Server for comment but did not receive a response before publication.

The reports come less than three months after BTCPay restricted remote Lightning access following an August attack that exploited a vulnerability to obtain Lightning Network Daemon (LND) credentials and steal funds.

It remains unclear whether the latest attack vector is connected to the August vulnerability.
